Negative appendectomy rates in children – A clinico-radio-pathological study: Category: GIT
ShresthaAshish LalDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Ashish Lal Shrestha/Kathmandu Medical College and Teaching Hospital, Sinamangal, Kathmandu, Nepal
E-mail: ashishlalshrestha75@gmail.com
Aims: The aim is to study the clinico-radio-pathological profile of children undergoing appendectomy at a tertiary center of Nepal.
Methods: A retrospective review of all the children undergoing appendectomy for suspected appendicitis between 2021 and 2022 by a single surgeon were done. Their clinicodemographical profile, laboratory and radiological findings and management plan were studied. This was matched with the histopathological findings to verify the accuracy of diagnosis.
Results: Sixty cases were identified. Of these preoperative diagnoses were made 58 based on history, clinical examination, blood counts, and ultrasonogram. In addition, CT abdomen was required in 5/60 for further confirmation. The intraoperative diagnosis was made in 2/60 based on the gross appearance of appendix. Of 60, 40 had laparoscopic and 20 had an open appendectomy. On histopathology, 10 were found to have gangrenous appendicitis, 22 had acute appendicitis, 23 had acute suppurative appendicitis, 1 had appendicular oxyuriasis, 2 had reactive lymphoid hyperplasia, 1 had a resolving appendicitis with fibrous obliteration, and 1 had a normal appendix. The overall negative appendectomy rate was 1.6%.
Conclusion: In children, accurate diagnosis of appendicitis requires cumulative information consisting of clinical findings, laboratory parameters, and ultrasonogram in most. In doubtful cases, the addition of a cross-sectional imaging can reduce the percentage of negative appendectomy.
Achalasia cardia – Case series: Category: GIT
MahalingamKokilavaniBalagopalSSundararajanLakshmiRamachandranPriyaNamashivayamSSrinivasSDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Kanchi Kamakoti CHILDS Trust Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: koksneurocard@gmail.com
Aim: Achalasia cardia is an esophageal motility disorder, characterized by the absence of lower esophageal sphincter relaxation and aperistalsis of esophagus. This can be due to primary/secondary causes.
We are presenting a series of 10 cases to highlight the presentation, syndromes/diseases associated, evaluation, and management of achalasia cardia.
Materials and Methods: Our study includes 10 cases of achalasia cardia over the past 10 years (9 cases in 7 years). Demographics, clinical presentation, investigations, management details, and outcome were collected retrospectively from case files.
Results: Among the 10 cases, all presented with dysphagia, vomiting, recurrent respiratory infection, and cough. Three patients presented additionally with alacrymia and hyperpigmentation of palms, lips-evaluated to be Triple A syndrome. One patient had associated Hodgkin's lymphoma and one other with Moya moya disease. All but one patient was evaluated with barium meal, high-resolution manometry and esophagogastroduodenoscopy. Management - 3 patients underwent open Heller's myotomy with fundoplication, 3 patients underwent laparoscopic repair, 2 underwent pneumatic dilatation, 1 managed medically, and 1 lost follow-up for management. Postsurgical repair, 2 patients required pneumatic dilatation, 1 patient required Botox injection. Other patients are doing well.
Conclusion: Achalasia cardia is an esophageal motility disorder, which presents with dysphagia and progressive disease can lead to failure to thrive and developmental delay. This can also be associated with syndromes. Detailed evaluation and early management can alleviate the symptoms and help in the thriving of the child.
Postoperative outcomes in pediatric patients undergoing laparoscopic appendectomy for acute appendicitis: Category: GIT
AithalShrilakshmiSaxenaRahulJadhavAvinashDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: shriaithal96@gmail.com
Introduction: Acute appendicitis is the most common surgical condition in children and there are two operative approaches: Laparoscopic and open appendectomy. Nowadays, with the advent of the instruments and optics, laparoscopic appendectomy has been found to be safer and effective in children.
Aim: The aim is to analyze the outcome of laparoscopic appendectomy in the pediatric age group with acute appendicitis.
Methods: Retrospective analysis of postoperative outcome of patients undergoing laparoscopic appendectomy in the Department of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, Jodhpur, between June 2016 and June 2022 was done. Data collected included intraoperative findings, postoperative complications within 30 days of surgery and management of complications, length of stay in the hospital.
Results: Out of 242 patients presenting with appendicitis, 220 (90%) patients underwent laparoscopic appendectomy, whereas 22 patients required open approach, in view of patients presenting with peritonitis and intestinal obstruction. 86 (39%) patients who underwent laparoscopic appendectomy had perforation, with localized or diffuse peritonitis. Twelve patients had port site infections and 7 patients had an adhesive intestinal obstruction in the immediate postoperative period requiring adhesiolysis. Other complications included postoperative ileus and pain abdomen. The median hospital stay following laparoscopic appendectomy was found to be 2 days.
Discussion: Laparoscopic technique seems to be safer with less complications and reduced duration of hospital stay among children with acute appendicitis.
Adhesive intestinal obstruction in pediatric patients following laparoscopic abdominal surgery: Category: GIT
AithalShrilakshmiSaxenaRahulPathakManishRathodKirtikumarJadhavAvinashNayakShubhalaxmiSinhaArvindDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: shriaithal96@gmail.com
Introduction: Adhesion formation is part of the healing process of the peritoneum following inflammation or trauma. Thus, abdominal surgery is a major cause of adhesion formation. These children can be managed conservatively in early stages, with up to 40%–60% requiring surgical intervention. The laparoscopic approach would be expected to be associated with decreased adhesion formation.
Aim: The aim is to analyze the incidence of intestinal obstruction in children following laparoscopic surgery and to determine the advantage of laparoscopic approach over the open approach.
Method: Retrospective analysis of the patients presenting to the Department of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS Jodhpur, with postoperative adhesive intestinal obstruction between June 2016 and June 2022 was done. Data collected included initial diagnosis, age at initial operation, initial surgical approach, time to develop intestinal obstruction, and type of definitive treatment.
Results: Seventy-seven children presented with postoperative intestinal obstruction, out of which up to 33 (43%) children required surgical intervention and remaining children were managed conservatively with bowel rest. Fifty eight (76%) of the patients had undergone laparoscopic surgery earlier, out of which 42 (73%) patients had perforated appendicitis. The time interval between the presentation was varying between 7 days and 4 years of the first surgery, with median interval of 3–5 months.
Discussion: The studies have shown less likelihood of obstruction following laparoscopic surgery, higher incidence in our study can be explained due to bias, as our institution is a tertiary center, with majority of the complicated cases being referred from other institutions. However, out of 220 patients who have underwent laparoscopic appendectomy in our institution, only 36 (16%) patients had postoperative intestinal obstruction.
Posterior Cloaca: A rare problem: Category: GIT
AnandAbhishekBanerjeeT JBasuA KGuptaPBhaumikKDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Institute Of Child Health, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: pandeyabhi13@live.com
Aims: The aim is to present the management of a unique anomaly in which urethra and vagina are fused together forming a urogenital sinus that deviated posteriorly and open in the anterior rectal wall at the anus.
Methods: Female baby presented on day 1 with a single orifice in the perineum at the location of anus, A lump in the abdomen and severe oliguria. Ultrasonography on day 1 showed distended urinary bladder and solitary right kidney. Vesicostomy was done. It failed to drain urine and a vaginostomy was performed on day 3. Vaginostomy drained urine and the renal biochemistry improved. A colostomy was performed before perineal operation. The perineal operation was performed at age of 1 year and 2 months. 3rd stage operation was done at 2 years 6 months during which cystoscopy and stenting were done. Vagina was made as a bladder after dissecting cervix from vagina and cervix attached to bowel opening into perineum and proximal bowel pulled down to make neoanus. Finally, colostomy is closed.
Results: Total mobilization of anorectum and urogenital sinus was done. The anus was placed at the striated muscle complex and urogenital sinus was shortened and placed at the introitus. Restoration of near normal anatomy achieved by making neovagina and rectum with native vagina functioning as a bladder
Conclusion: Such complicated anomaly requires well-planned staged repair. Total urogenital mobilization should be performed preserving the anorectum in its normal location.
Ruptured rectal duplication cyst with ectopic left pelvic kidney mimicking sacrococcygeal teratoma: Category: GIT
ReddyP Manoj KumarRaoN BhuvaneshwarKotaiahMandakiniDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Niloufer Hospital, Hyderabad, Telangana, India
E-mail: manojj.4892007@gmail.com
Introduction: Intestinal duplications are rare developmental anomalies that can occur anywhere along the gastrointestinal tract. Rectal duplication cysts account for approximately 4% of all duplication cysts. Due to its rarity, the difficulty of making a correct diagnosis, and of selection of proper approach for treatment, this entity bears a special significance.
Presentation of the Case: A 5-day-old female baby was brought to us after birth, with a mucosa-lined left hemi perineal lesion mimicking sacrococcygeal teratoma. On examination, noted an irregular lobulated mass over the sacral region with mucosal line blind-ending tract. External genitalia and anal opening appeared normal. Computed tomography Scan was suggestive of left ectopic pelvic kidney herniating through the left obturator foramen to the left gluteal region along with some soft tissue into the subcutaneous plane.
Perineal anatomy was restored by excising the mucosa-lined lesion and left kidney visualized underneath intraoperatively. HPE showed single blind-ending tubular tissue, lined by colonic epithelium showing crypts and glands, focal respiratory epithelium.
Discussion: Our case is unique as the combination of a rectal duplication (which was ruptured antenatally) with associated ectopic left pelvic kidney with ptosis into the gluteal region was not described earlier.
The other described cases in the literature with ruptured RD were associated with exstrophy, hypospadias, and anorectal malformation.
Conclusion: Rectal duplication is a very rare type of alimentary tract malformation and newborn presentation with rupture and ectopic left kidney in gluteal region is exceptional and difficult to diagnose.

GAshrith ReddyPhiliposeRashmi TDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Niloufer Hospital, Hyderabad, Telangana, India
E-mail: ashrtihgowni@gmail.com
Meckel's diverticulum is the most common developmental anomaly of the gastrointestinal tract. This anomaly is caused by incomplete obliteration of the omphalomesenteric duct. Meckel's diverticulum is usually seen as an incidental finding during a laparotomy for other causes, such as acute appendicitis.
Objective: The aim of this study is to present an extremely rare case of double Meckel's diverticulum presenting as acute abdomen.
Methods: A 5-year-old female child was admitted to emergency with symptoms of acute right lower abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting. With a presumed diagnosis of acute appendicitis McBurney's incision was made. Surgical exploration revealed a double Meckel's diverticulum located approximately 45 to 50 cm proximal to the ileocecal valve, appendix was found to be normal intraoperatively. Conclusions: In patients with acute abdomen clinic, Meckel's diverticulum and its complications should be kept in mind, and the intestines should be observed for an extra diverticulum for caution although it is a very rare condition

E-mail: anjanakaza@gmail.com
Background: Appendicitis and appendicular perforations frequently present in the pediatric age group. Although appendicitis has some certain symptoms, they might present with unusual symptoms in the pediatric age group leading to a diagnostic dilemma.
Case Report: A 4 year-old female child presented with painful micturition for 10 days and history of fever 12 days back. Computed tomography abdomen and pelvis revealed mixed attenuation lesion in the presacral region causing compression on bladder, and the possibility of a teratoma was suspected. Intraoperative findings included appendicular mass due to a ruptured pelvic appendix. Postoperative imaging showed no residual mass.
Conclusion: Atypical anatomical variations of inflamed appendix can mimic symptoms of urinary tract infection/urinary obstruction. A rare case report of Hirschsprung's disease presenting as acute appendicitis: Category: GIT
BhargavL SaiNarayanaK V SDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Niloufer hospital, Hyderabad, Telangana, India
E-mail: dr.saibhargav@gmail.com
Introduction: Acute appendicitis is the most common diagnosis in children. In case of perforated appendix, The Symptoms are much more obvious With systemic signs.
Objective: Aim of this study is to present an extremely rare case of Hirschsprung's disease presenting as acute appendicitis.
Methods: We report a case of 5 year male child who presented to us with constipation and abdominal distention and vomiting. At presentation, the child was dehydrated with abdominal signs suggestive of peritonitis.in view of suspected diagnosis of appendicular perforation laparotomy was done. Laparotomy revealed dilated large bowel. Rectal biopsy was performed to rule out underlying Hirschsprung's disease. Histopathologic examination of appendiceal mucosa showed necrosis and inflammatory infiltration with neutrophils. Muscularis and serosa were normal. Ganglion cells were seen within the appendix but were absent in rectal biopsy.
Conclusion: Hirschsprung's disease can rarely lead to appendicitis and appendicular perforation. Therefore, it is important to have a high index of suspicion and should be included in the differential diagnosis of a child or an infant presenting with an acute appendicitis with vague signs and symptoms.
Disconnected pancreatic duct syndrome: A case report: Category: HEPATO BILIARY
KrishnanNellaiMukherjeeSampreetiDhuaAnjanYadavDevendra KumarGoelPrabudhBajpaiMinuMalikRohanDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to present a rare case of disconnected pancreatic duct syndrome (DPDS) managed successfully.
Case Reports: A 12-year-old female presented with recurrent episodes of severe abdomen pain and vomiting of 4 months’ duration. She was admitted, evaluated, and diagnosed with idiopathic necrotizing pancreatitis. She developed left hydropneumothorax, which did not resolve with tube thoracostomy drainage. Thoracoscopic pleural toilet and drainage were undertaken. After a brief period of transient improvement, she developed a sizeable peripancreatic collection for which she underwent necrosectomy and abdominal drain placement. Her general condition showed some improvement, but she developed a persistent controlled pancreatic fistula. During the same time, she developed a life-threatening upper gastrointestinal bleed which was attributed to a ruptured gastroduodenal vessel in the duodenum and was managed by interventional radiology procedure. The pancreatic fistula made us order an MRCP that showed features of a DPDS for which internal drainage (tractor-jejunostomy) procedure was performed for DPDS. Subsequently, the patient had an uneventful recovery and was discharged after 129 days. She is currently asymptomatic after 4 months of discharge.
Conclusion: DPDS can be a rare complication of necrotizing pancreatitis with a prolonged course to recovery and requiring multimodality therapeutic approaches with unconventional surgical procedures such as tractor-jejunostomy done in our case.
Portal vein web – A rare cause of extra-hepatic portal vein obstruction in a child: Category: HEPATO BILIARY
ChackoBenjaminNabeelQZameerM MVinayCRaoSanjayD’cruzAshley JDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Narayana Health Hospitals, Bangalore, Karnataka, India
E-mail: benjaminchacko25@gmail.com
Aim: Extra-hepatic portal vein obstruction (EHPVO) secondary to portal vein thrombosis is the most common cause of portal hypertension in children. Congenital portal vein anomalies causing EHPVO, especially portal vein web are very rare etiology. We report a case of portal vein web which was managed successfully by portal venoplasty.
Case Summary: A 3-year-old girl presented with abdominal distension and easy bruising for 2 months. On evaluation, she was found to have hypersplenism, pancytopenia, with grade II esophageal varices. Triphasic contrast-enhanced computed tomography revealed narrowing of the portal vein just proximal to the porta, raising a suspicion of portal web. Due to her age and favorable left portal vein, Rex shunt using a left IJV graft was created. Unfortunately, she developed early shunt thrombosis and underwent re-exploration. The thrombosed Rex shunt was taken down. The portal vein was dissected up to portal plate and flow to the left and right branches were controlled. PV was slit open and augmented using Rex shunt vein graft. Postoperatively, she was started on anticoagulant: Dalteparin, which was later, transitioned to aspirin. On 1-year follow-up, a child is doing well. Follow-up Doppler shows a patent portal vein and the hypersplenism had resolved
Conclusion: Portal vein web, though rare, is a cause for EHPVO. It can be surgically corrected by a portal venoplasty using an autograft. Although a complex and challenging surgery, it is curative, spleen preserving, and more so preserves the natural portal anatomy.
Choledochal cyst and Annular Pancreas: A rare combination of malformations!!!: Category: HEPATO BILIARY
AhmadFahimMalikMuneer ASamujhRamDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, PGIMER, Chandigarh, India
E-mail: fahimrockz90@gmail.com
Aim: Choledochal cyst (CDC) and annular pancreas are embryologically closely related entities; however, there have been only a limited number of case reports. Knowledge of both the presentation and management of this rare co-occurrence is imperative in avoiding potential complications and sequelae. Herein, we describe a 2-year female who was diagnosed with type 1 CDC and was planned for laparoscopic procedure. Intraoperatively, she was found to have an annular pancreas, which is a rare association with CDC.
Methods: A 2-year-old female child who had presented with recurrent attacks of abdominal pain for the past 6 months, requiring multiple admissions. Ultrasonography and MRCP abdomen were suggestive of CDC type 1. She was planned for laparoscopic CDC excision and hepaticoduodenostomy (HD). Four ports 10 + 5 + 5 + 5 were used. Intraoperatively, annular pancreas was found so, the decision was changed and hence, CDC excision and hepaticojejunostomy were done.
Results: Patient underwent laparoscopic CDC excision and ROUX-en-Y-Hepaticojejunostomy uneventfully. She recovered well and discharged on postoperative day 6 and is symptomatically relieved now.
Conclusion: The simultaneous occurrence of CDC and annular pancreas, although rare, has common anatomical findings. This case highlights the importance of awareness of these rare associations, leading to change in the management plan. The definitive surgical single-stage procedure of multiples anomalies in children by laparoscopy, though challenging can be safely performed in pediatric surgical centers with adequate pediatric anesthesia.
Biliary atresia associated with colonic atresia: Category: HEPATO BILIARY
AggarwalKomalMahajanJ KSolankiShaileshMalikMuneer AbasSamujhRamDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Post Graduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, India
E-mail: komalaggarwal57@gmail.com
Introduction: Several congenital anomalies have been reported in association with biliary atresia (BA). While small bowel atresia has been reported to co-occur with BA, association with colonic atresia (CA) is very rare. We report a case of CA co-existing with BA.
Case Report: A 6-day female child presented in emergency with a complaint of feed intolerance, nonbilious vomiting, abdominal distension, normally placed anal orifice, and failure to pass meconium since birth. On examination, the child had gross abdominal distension and X-ray abdomen showed distal bowel obstruction. After resuscitation, the baby was explored and two CAs, one at the level of the mid-transverse colon and another at the proximal sigmoid colon were found. For distal colonic atresia, resection and end-to-end anastomosis was done while colostomy and mucus fistula was created for proximal atresia. It was noticed that intraluminal content was pale in color with no bile stain but was no proximal intestinal atresia. Inspection of porta hepatis showed a small and shrunken gall bladder; a liver biopsy was taken before closure. Postoperative course was uneventful; however, the stoma output was clay-colored. The liver biopsy was inconclusive. A HIDA scan at 4 weeks of age showed the absence of enterohepatic circulation. Kasai portoenterostomy was performed on day 60 of life. The postoperative course was uneventful, and the child was passing green stool from the colostomy. At 6 months of follow-up, the child is passing normal color stool and is waiting for stoma closure.
Conclusion: Both colonic and BA are uncommon types of anomalies. Timely diagnosis of such uncommon association at initial presentation provides a good outcome.
Congenital intrahepatic portosystemic shunt – A surgical challenge: Category: HEPATO BILIARY
NayakShubhalaxmiSinhaAravindRathodKirti KumarKattiMadhusudanKheraPushpinderDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: drshubhanyk@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to report the challenges faced in the management of an extremely rare case of congenital intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (PSS).
Case Report: A 10-year-old boy presented with complaints of poor weight gain and progressive exertional dyspnea. Clinical examination revealed a pan systolic murmur and a normal physical and systemic examination. Ultrasonography abdomen was suggestive of an abnormal shunt between the main portal vein and inferior vena cava. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography abdomen confirmed the diagnosis of congenital intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (Type 2b). Biochemical investigations were normal. Bubble 2D echo was suggestive of extra-cardiac shunt. A diagnostic portogram revealed 24 mm PSS along with hypoplastic bilobar intrahepatic portal venous branches. Balloon occlusion of the shunt demonstrated an acceptable increase in portal venous pressure (23–30 mmHg). Endovascular coiling of the shunt was attempted, however, due to the large dimension of the shunt, the procedure was terminated and planned for surgery.
Owing to the rarity of the disease and unusual location of the shunt, a cadaveric dissection was initially performed. The child underwent exploratory laparotomy through a thoracoabdominal approach with backup for cardiac bypass. The right lobe of the liver was mobilized and reflected medially to expose the entire length of retrohepatic IVC. The PSS was identified and isolated. After adequately controlling the supra shunt (balloon catheter) and infra shunt IVC, the shunt was divided from the IVC and repaired. No intraoperative signs of portal hypertension were identified. Postoperatively, the child is asymptomatic.
Conclusion: Surgical management of CIHPSS is challenging and requires appropriate preoperative workup and intraoperative planning and dexterity.
Learning curves of common minimally invasive pediatric urology procedures: A systematic review: Category: MIS
KrishnanNellaiDhuaAnjanJainVisheshAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Aim: No consensus concerning the minimum number of operations needed to become proficient in minimally invasive surgical procedures (MIS) in pediatric urology. The aim is to systematically summarize the learning curve of common MIS in pediatric urology.
Methods: The authors systematically searched the databases PubMed, EMBASE, and Scopus using appropriate keywords. The eligibility criteria were studies on the learning curve in pediatric laparoscopic or robotic-assisted pyeloplasty, nephrectomy, and ureteral reimplantation. Operating time and postoperative complications during and after learning curve were assessed. The quality assessment of the studies was done using the Downs and Black scale.
Results: Eight studies were included in the review. The presentation of learning curve was using a line graph in 6/8 (75%) studies, cumulative summation analysis in 1/8 (12.5%) study, and no graphical representation in 1/8 (12.5%) study. In most of the studies (7/8; 87%), patients were arbitrarily split into groups and analyzed. The learning curve reported was 10–16 in laparoscopic nephrectomy, 20–58 in robot-assisted laparoscopic pyeloplasty, and 13 in transvesicoscopic ureteral reimplantation. The decrease in operative time after the learning curve was present in all the studies. Five studies also reported a decrease in complication rate after the learning curve. There was a moderate risk of bias in all studies.
Conclusions: The learning curve does exist in MIS in pediatric urology. No consensus on the exact length of learning curve.
Laparoscopic approach in the treatment of palpable cryptorchidism: A pooled analysis: Category: MIS
KrishnanNellaiPogorelicZenonAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Aims: The present pooled analysis aimed to investigate the utility of laparoscopic orchiopexy for palpable cryptorchidism.
Methods: Four scientific databases (PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science, and EMBASE) were systematically searched for relevant articles using the following terms: palpable cryptorchidism or palpable undescended testes AND laparoscopic orchiopexy or laparoscopic orchiopexy. The inclusion criteria were all children with unilateral or bilateral palpable undescended testes who underwent laparoscopic orchiopexy (LO) compared to children who underwent conventional open orchiopexy (CO). The main outcomes were the proportion of children requiring redo-orchiopexy and the incidence of postoperative complications. Secondary outcomes were duration and the cost of surgery.
Results: The final analysis included five studies involving 705 children; LO, n = 369 (52.3%) and CO, n = 336 (47.7%). The majority of the included patients had unilateral palpable cryptorchidism. No significant differences were found in regard to average age at the time of surgery and follow-up periods between the investigated groups. No statistically significant differences were found in regard to redo-orchiopexy rates (relative risk [RR] = 0.22, 95% confidence interval [CI] 0.03–1.88, P = 0.17), early complications (RR = 0.66, 95% CI 0.21–2.08, P = 0.48), and incidence of testicular atrophy (RR = 0.36, 95% CI 0.03–3.88, P = 0.40). No significant differences in the operative duration were observed among the groups. Laparoscopy was associated with higher costs in most of the studies.
Conclusion: The rates of redo-orchiopexy as well as an incidence of early complications and testicular atrophy rates in LO are comparable to CO.
Barbed versus nonbarbed suture for pyeloplasty through the minimally invasive approach – A systemic review and met analysis: Category: MIS
JadhavShishirJukicMiroKrishnanNellaiPogorelicZenonAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, New Delhi, India
E-mail: jadhavshishir03@gmail.com
Aim: The aim of this meta-analysis was to summarize and analyze current evidence on suturing mate rials for laparoscopic pyeloplasty.
Materials and Methods: This study was conducted in accordance with the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses guidelines. Scientific databases (PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science, and EMBASE) were systematically searched for relevant comparative studies on barbed suture (BS) and non BS (NBS) in minimally invasive pyeloplasty.
Results: Five comparative studies met the inclusion criteria and were included in the meta-analysis. Pooling the data demonstrated a significantly shorter operative duration in patients belonging to the BS group versus the NBS group (95% confidence interval [CI]-81.63 to-10.41, P = 0.01), with statistically significant estimated heterogeneity among the included studies (P < 0.0001). Although the incidence of redo-pyeloplasty was higher in the BS group, the pooled risk ratio (RR) for the need for redo-pyeloplasty in subjects belonging to the BS group versus the NBS group was 6.00 (95% CI 0.78–46.14), demonstrating no statistically significant difference (P = 0.09). A total of 5 patients developed postoperative complications. The pooled RR for the occurrence of these complications showed no significant difference among the patients belonging to both the groups (95% CI 0.22–6.05, P =.88).
Conclusions: Minimally invasive pyeloplasty using BS is associated with significantly shorter operative time. The incidence of postoperative complications and requirement of redo-pyeloplasty showed no significant difference among both the treatment groups. Further randomized controlled trials need to be conducted before any definite conclusions are drawn.
Laparoscopic-intraperitoneal onlay mesh repair of multiple abdomen-wall ventral hernias: Category: MIS
SinhaC KElfarHazemSaeedAtifMukhtarZahidDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, St Georges University Hospital, London
E-mail: cksinha11@gmail.com
Aim: The incidence of multiple abdominal-wall ventral hernias in children is rare in comparison to adults. In children, primary closure has generally been favored. However, synthetic or biomedical mesh offers advantages in the repair of recurrent, large and multiple defects, when primary closure is difficult. The aim was to use Laparoscopic-IntraPeritoneal Onlay Mesh (IPOM) for abdomen-wall ventral hernias repair. There is no similar report in children.
Methods: A 13-year-old severely obese girl presented with recurrent abdominal pain for few years. She has a past history of open Spigelian hernia, repaired 2 years before her current presentation. She was also being investigated for suspected connective tissue disorder. On examination, her body weight was 120 kg, and BMI 45. Abdominal examination was difficult due to obesity, but a few nontender soft swellings were felt in the midline.
Magnetic resonance imaging was performed, which showed multiple midline ventral hernias.
Results: In the background of obesity and multiple defects, a laparoscopic-IPOM repair was planned. The fascial defects were detected precisely through the laparoscopy, and nonabsorbable mesh was placed through a 12-mm trocar. Minimal incisions were required for the trocars, and extensive dissection of the abdominal wall structure was not required. This procedure allowed us in maintaining the anatomical integrity and functional status of the abdominal wall muscles. The postoperative recovery was quick and uneventful. In follow-up, the patient is doing well and free of abdomen pain with minimal scarring and excellent cosmetic appearance.
Conclusion: Although mesh hernia repair is not preferred in the pediatric age group, laparoscopic-IPOM repair could be an easy and preferred operation for multiple abdominal-wall ventral hernias, especially in recurrent, multiple, and obese cases.
Thoracoscopic management of Corrosive esophageal strictures: Category: MIS
MalviyaSonalPSVSBVGRKMKPMDPKADMSDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Seth G S and KEM hospital Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: sonal.malviyaigmc@gmail.com
Introduction: Short-segment corrosive esophageal strictures may be managed with resection of the affected segment and primary anastomosis of esophageal ends. Here, we present two such cases with caustic esophageal strictures who had gastrostomy initially.
Case 1: A 6-year-old girl with corrosive short segment stricture was confirmed with contrast swallow. Laparotomy with distal Collis gastroplasty was done to gain distal esophageal length. End-to-side esophago-esophageal anastomosis was attempted using circular stapler passed per abdomen and anvil orally. This was done under thoracoscopic guidance. Failure to get a full circle of doughnut resulted in open thoracotomy and completion of hand-sewn anastomosis with excision of stricture segment.
Case 2: A 4-year-old male child with upper GI series confirming corrosive short segment stricture length. Thoracoscopic resection of short strictured segment with end-to-end anastomosis was done. Both patients are on 9 months’ follow-up postoperatively, on semi-solid food, gaining weight, and on regular upper GI endoscopy for dilatation.
Conclusion: Thoracoscopic resection and anastomosis of strictured esophageal segment with preservation of native esophagus with esophageal lengthening procedures such as collis gastroplasty is safe and possible.
Laparoscopic management of nonpalpable testis: 6-year single-center experience – a retrospective descriptive study: Category: MIS
KrishnamurthyShreyasSaxenaRahulPathakManishRathodKirtikumarJadhavAvinashNayakShubhalaxmiSinhaArvindDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: shreyas.m23@gmail.com
Introduction: Undescended testis is one of the most common urological conditions encountered by pediatric surgeons. About 20% of undescended testes are impalpable. Among these cases, there is a significant possibility of malignancy in these testes, absence of one or both the testes which can later impact the fertility of these patients.
Methods: We retrospectively analyzed the demographic and clinical findings, as well as immediate and 6-month outcomes, in 88 patients diagnosed with impalpable undescended testes between July 2016 and June 2022. Outcome variables were the location of the testis on diagnostic laparoscopy, number of stages involved, patients with bilateral undescended testes, cases requiring orchidectomy or nubbin excision, position achieved at the end of the surgery, and basis of decision for staged repair. In follow-up, we looked for the location of the testis, testicular growth or atrophy, and the number of patients. The complications reported during the surgery like injury to the vas, and scrotal hematoma were also recorded.
Results: Of the 88 patients, 17 had bilateral and 71 cases had unilateral impalpable testes. All 105 testes were managed laparoscopically. While most of the unilateral impalpable testes underwent successful laparoscopy-assisted orchiopexy in single stage, 8 cases of bilateral impalpable testes were managed in single stage, 8 in two stages, and 1 case required 3 staged orchiopexy.
Conclusion: Laparoscopy is extremely useful in both the diagnosis and treatment of impalpable testes. Objectively measured mobility of the testis toward the contralateral internal inguinal ring is an excellent intraoperative indicator for the type of orchiopexy. Standardization of management may increase the success rate of orchiopexy.
Outcomes of testicular torsion among children presenting during the coronavirus disease 2019 pandemic: A pooled analysis: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
KrishnanNellaiPogorelicZenonArtukovicLeonAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Aims: The aims is to evaluate whether the Coronavirus Disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic resulted in a prolonged duration of symptoms, a delayed presentation to the medical facility and consequently more orchiectomy procedures among children with testicular torsion compared to the pre-COVID-19 period.
Methods: Systematic search of four scientific databases was performed with appropriate search terms. The inclusion criteria were all boys presenting with testicular torsion during the COVID-19 and pre-COVID-19 periods. A comparison of the average duration of symptoms, the proportion of children with delayed presentation (>24 h), and the proportion of children requiring orchiectomy was made among the two groups.
Results: The analysis included six comparative studies (five retrospective studies). A total of 711 patients (473 during the COVID-19 period) were included. No significant differences in the average duration of symptoms (WMD: 2.6, 95% confidence interval [CI]-6.78–11.99, P = 0.59), the proportion of children with delayed presentation (risk ratio [RR] = 1.03, 95% CI 0.52–2.02, P = 0.94), and orchiectomy rate (RR = 1.23, 95% CI 0.82–1.84, P = 0.31) were observed among the two patient groups. All studies had a moderate risk of bias.
Conclusion: The duration of symptoms, the proportion of children with delayed presentation, and orchiectomy rate did not significantly differ among the children with testicular torsion presenting during the COVID-19 and pre-COVID-19 periods. However, due to the moderate risk of bias, the level of evidence in the available comparative studies is limited.
A rare case of congenital macroglossia: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
ChandKaruneshDaveraSaketDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Army Hospital (RR), New Delhi, India
E-mail: karuneshshahi@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to present a rare case of congenital macroglossia due to glial choristoma of tongue which presented as a growing tongue lesion, with surgical challenges concerning complete excision of lesion along with preservation and reconstruction of the tongue.
Methods: A 5-month-old male infant presented with a congenital, progressively increasing lesion in the tongue completely filling the oral cavity and overhanging outside the oral cavity. Sucking and swallowing were poor. No history of stridor or resp distress. Examination revealed large lesion occupying almost the whole of the tongue. In view of the grotesque appearance and difficulty in feeding the child was planned for surgery. The patient underwent a complete excision of the lesion. Per operative findings revealed 6 cm × 5 cm soft mass occupying the whole of tongue, sparing a small sliver of normal tongue tissue all around.
Result: The patient had an uneventful recovery. The child was fed through NG tube for a week and thereafter he was started on breastfeed. The patient is on follow-up and is doing well with adequate sucking and swallowing and normal tongue movements. Histopathology was suggestive of glial Choristoma.
Discussion: Glial Choristoma comprises a type of brain heterotopia with normal central nervous tissue in an abnormal location. Glial Choristoma presenting as a tongue lesion is very rare and is associated with breathing and feeding problems. Surgical resection is the standard treatment for glial choristoma. The optimal timing of surgery in the neonatal period is, however debatable. Early resection enables early extubation and oral feeding. The small size of the organ and the narrow operating field hamper the surgical approach.
Conclusion: Tongue is a rare location of congenital heterotopia of brain tissue. It is treatable with surgical excision. Complete excision is the key to ensure complete removal of the lesion, as well as to salvage adequate tongue tissue for satisfactory cosmetic and functional outcome.
Pilomatrixoma: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
BanerjeeArkaBendrePradnya SMunghateGayatriTiwariYogesh ADepartment of Pediatric Surgery, B J Wadia Hospital for Children, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: arkabanerjee6989@gmail.com
Introduction: Pilomatrixoma is a benign skin tumor that develops from the hair follicle matrix cells. It represents around 0.1% of all skin tumors. Although pilomatrixomas are well-recognized lesions, clinically, they are frequently misdiagnosed as other skin conditions, most likely due to a lack of awareness by clinicians. We present a series of 4 cases of pediatric pilomatrixoma.
Objectives: The objective is to increase awareness and thereby decrease difficulties in the diagnosis of pilomatrixomas in children as well as add to the growing literature on this benign skin tumor.
Methods: The medical records of 5 children with histologically diagnosed pilomatrixoma between January 2018 and May 2022 were retrospectively evaluated.
Results: Five children (3 boys and 2 girls) with pilomatrixoma were reviewed retrospectively. The median age was 8 (4–11) years. The common complaint was a painless swelling that was immobile and fixed to the overlying skin. There was no notable family history. All but one patient had solitary lesions and all were in the head-and-neck region. The size of the lesions ranged from 0.8 cm to 1.5 cm (median 1.5 cm). All the patients had their lesions excised completely and sent for histopathological examination which confirmed the diagnosis of a pilomatrixoma. After a median follow-up of 8 (3–-41.5) months, none of our patients developed any recurrence or malignancy.
Conclusion: Pilomatrixoma is an uncommon benign skin tumor, sometimes with atypical forms and no tumor-specific diagnostic feature except a careful histopathological examination. However, it is easily excisable, and recurrence is rare.
Spectrum of antenatally diagnosed congenital anomalies and their postnatal management at tertiary health center in central India: Category: MISCELLENEOUS

E-mail: drsunitasingh28@gmail.com
Aim: Congenital malformation is rapidly emerging as one of the major worldwide problems. Our aim is to study the demography and risk factors in pregnancy suspected of structural birth defects with a study of the spectrum of anomalies and their postnatal management.
Material and Methods: Retrospective record analysis of outpatient department referral of pregnancy having antenatal suspicion of congenital malformation from 2014 to 2019 was done. Those were followed throughout the pregnancy and findings at termination or postnatal delivery were recorded.
Results: A total of 350 antenatal suspected malformation records were identified. 127 patients did not turn up once counseling regarding malformation in fetus was done with parents. Central nervous system (CNS) malformation were most common. One hundred and five patients were referred to a center having a facility of genetic testing or fetal intervention. Fifty-one major anomalies that were not compatible with life and <20 weeks were advised for termination of pregnancy. Renal pyelectesis was the most common abnormality followed by CNS. 67 pregnancies were delivered live at our center. 25 underwent surgery, 8 expired postoperatively. Nonoperative deaths were 5. 15 refused treatment and lost the follow-up. 22 patients were managed conservatively. Congenital diaphragmatic hernia (14/25), abdominal wall defect (4/25), ovarian cyst (2/25), mesenteric cyst (1/25), duodenal atresia (1/25), esophageal fistula with tracheoesophageal fistula (1/25), meningomyelocele (1/25), CCAM (1/25) were operative cases.
Conclusion: Proper periconception care, prenatal screening, and postnatal care of malformed fetus can decrease the long-term mortality and morbidity in tiny sufferers.

Aim: The aim is to study the rare tumors of supra renal gland.

Introduction: Cyst of the adrenal gland is rare and is usually an incidental finding. Large cyst of the adrenal in an infant usually point toward a cystic neuroblastoma. Cystic neuroblastoma lesions even though rare neoplasm usually present as a prenatally diagnosed cystic mass and is usually seen in infancy. This is a case of a 6-month-old female child presented with a cystic lesion on the left side of abdomen. The initial clinical diagnosis was a Wilms’ Tumor. Sclerotherapy and surgery form the mainstay of treatment. However, in some complex situations, neither serve as a feasible option. Sirolimus directly inhibits the mammalian target of rapamycin that acts as a master switch in angio/lymphangiogenesis. Case reports and series have reported the successful use of sirolimus in children, but no consensus has been reached. We present our series of children with complex vascular anomalies treated with sirolimus.

A novel technique of umbilicoplasty in abdominoplasty of classic exstrophy bladder: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
SinghGurmeetPriyadarshiniApalaKureelS NGuptaArchikaGuptaSurvesh KumarTyagiNirpexDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, King George's Medical University, Lucknow (UP)
E-mail: gurmeet.dr@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to report a new technique of umbilicoplasty for enhanced esthetics of the abdominal wall in the surgery of bladder exstrophy.
Methods: From August 2020 to April 2022, a new technique was applied in seven cases of bladder exstrophy (three patients among these had a failed umbicoplasty earlier with the trapezoid flap technique). At the level of the transtubercular line in midline, umblicoplasty flap is marked in a rectangle of 27 mm (length) × 15 mm (width). The rectangle is further divided into three equal parts (cranial, middle, and caudal) of 9 mm x 15 mm each. In the caudal rectangle, the trapezoid flap is marked based on the middle rectangle. An oval shape is carved in middle rectangle based on upper rectangle. Cranial segment is marked as a reverse trapezoid based on transtubercular line. Overall, a dumbbell shape flap is created based on transtubercular line. The flap is raised along with scarpa fascia and left attached at transtubercular line. The middle oval flap is folded on itself and sutured to create a button-like structure. The button is anchored to transtubercular line. The caudal end of trapezoid is flipped up and margins anchored to upper end of cranial trapezoid, which is just enough to allow peeping of the button hole thus created. The lateral-free margin of abdominal wall are joined with each other and to margin of flipped lower flap. The structure thus created is identical to a normal umbilicus with central button and perinodular groove surrounded by skin bridge. The suture material used was 5-0-polyglactin 910 and the outcome was measured by resemblance to normal umbilicus over a scale of 1–10 and complications over a follow-up of 1 year.
Results: No postoperative complication/dehiscence. The resemblance with a normal umbilicus ranges from 8 to 9.
Conclusion: The described technique of umbilicoplasty is novel, without complication or dehiscence, and creates a normal-looking umbilicus.
Role of neurophysiological monitoring in improving outcome during separation of pyopagus conjoined twins: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
SehgalMehakGoelPrabudhJaryalAshok KumarYadavDevendra KumarJainVisheshDhuaAnjan KumarAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: mehak.sehgal91@gmail.com
Introduction and Aim: Separating the spinal cord while separating spine-sharing conjoined twins may have long-lasting implications for the survivors. We aim to study the role of neurophysiological monitoring in delineating the spinal anatomy as an important component of preoperative planning and twin separation, thereby improving neurological outcome.
Material and Methods: Prospective study based on 2 pairs of pyopagus conjoined twins with a shared spine including the spinal cord. The twins were subjected to neurophysiological monitoring under general anesthesia to identify the neuronal supply and cross-innervation of various organs, including lower limbs and anal sphincters, with respective brains.
Results: Preoperatively, the spinal cord was seen to be joined terminally with varying degrees in both the set of twins. Neurophysiological monitoring done preoperatively on the first set of twins revealed cross innervation from twin B to twin A, although twin A had its independent complete innervation. The sphincter was predominantly controlled by twin A. In the second set of twins, there was no cross-innervation, both twins had equal control of sphincter; hence, the sphincter allocation was done using other general and anatomical factors. Neurophysiological monitoring was also utilized intraoperatively to divide the cord in its functional midline instead of anatomical midline thereby preserving neural outflow and function. Postoperatively, both sets of twins retained motor and sensory supply to lower limbs and mobilized both limbs well, aided with physiotherapy.
Conclusion: Better functional outcomes are possible with the use of neurophysiological monitoring during the workup and management of spine-sharing conjoined twins.
A rare case of prepubertal ovarian abscess presenting as an acute abdomen: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
JagdishBYhoshuEnonoBalijaSatya SreeAhmedIntezarPiplaniRajatGuptaManish KumarDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, Rishikesh, Uttarakhand, India
E-mail: jagdish.bala1996@gmail.com
Introduction: Ovarian abscess is common in sexually active women of reproductive age secondary to pelvic inflammatory disease (PID). It is believed to be the result of an ascending genital tract infection. It is uncommon to present with an ovarian abscess in prepubertal girls. The youngest reported case in literature is a girl of 7 years (Journal of Pediatric and Adolescent Gynaecology 2021).
Case Report: A 6-year-old female presented to the pediatric ER with complaints of lower abdominal pain, fever, and vomiting along with associated burning micturition. On examination, a vague abdominal tender lump was palpable in the hypogastrium and RIF. Counts were raised with neutrophilia. Tumor markers were normal. ultrasonography showed a hyperechoic solid cystic mass lesion in the right adnexa with internal vascularity with few hyperechoic foci suggestive of an infective etiology. On contrast-enhanced computed tomography a well-defined thick-walled hypodense lesion was identified, with fluid and air pockets in the right adnexa suggestive of inflammatory pathology. On exploratory laparotomy, a right ovarian abscess was found which was opened, debrided, and drained. Histopathology of the abscess wall showed features of dense acute and chronic inflammation with no evidence of granuloma or malignancy. Pus culture was sterile, and CBNAAT was negative for tuberculosis. A 3-month follow-up revealed no residual collection on ultrasonography.
Conclusion: Ovarian abscess management should start as early as possible. Delay in management can lead to complications such as peritonitis, adhesions, and infertility. A high degree of suspicion is important in prepubertal females with an ovarian mass.
Ovarian torsion in children: Retrospective study in a single institute: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
JaseelP P MuhammedBALAGOPALSDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Kanchi Kamakoti Childs Trust Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: mjaseelpp@gmail.com
Aim: The purpose of this study is to evaluate clinical symptoms diagnosis, management, and outcomes in children with ovarian torsion
Design and Participants: All surgically managed cases of ovarian torsion in a single institute from 2012 to 2022 were retrospectively reviewed.
Results: A total of 25 girls were identified out of which three cases were antenatally detected and operated in infancy. In the remaining 22 cases, the median age was 11 years. No recurrences were noted during the study. The main presenting symptoms were abdominal pain in all children (100%) and vomiting (68%). Laparoscopy was performed in 13 (52%) cases, laparotomy in 12 (48%) cases. Cyst noted in 13 (52%) cases out of which cyst excision with preservation of ovary done for 5 cases. The total number of oophorectomies was 10 (40%). Oust of 15 ovary preserving cases, ultrasonography follow-up was done for 13 (86%) cases, all showed normal looking ovary. Opposite side oopheropexy was done for 12 (50%) cases.
Conclusions: Abdominal pain was the main presenting symptom of ovarian torsion and most of the girls were above 5 years of age. The mode of surgery depended on surgeons’ preference to individual cases. Equal number of open and minimally invasive surgery noted on records and the outcomes were equally same. Follow-up of ovaries treated by detorsion and left in the abdominal cavity preserved their normal anatomy in follow-up scans.
Sertoli only cell syndrome – A varying presentation: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
TinkeyLRengarajanRDharmendraR PDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Coimbatore Medical College Hospital, Coimbatore, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: tinkeykrishnan0491@gmail.com
Sertoli cell-only syndrome is also known as Del Castillo syndrome and germ cell aplasia. Sertoli cells are found in the convoluted seminiferous tubules and form part of the blood-testis barrier. They also play an important role in spermatogenesis. Sertoli cells also secrete the anti-Mullerian hormone AMH, which leads to the regression of Mullerian ducts in a male fetus. Sertoli cells also secrete inhibin-B and Activin necessary for feedback mechanisms.
We had operated 2 cases with bilateral undescended testes and gonads that look like ovo testes. Pole to Pole biopsy done showed sertoli only cells with the absence of Leidig cells. The aim of the study is to discuss the varying presentation and reviewing the literature
A rare case of nasal glial heterotopia in an infant: Case report: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
KalagotlaChandrikaAggarwalSatish KumarMangalMaheshDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: drkchandrika2k9@gmail.com
Introduction: Glial heterotopia is a rare congenital lesion composed of dysplastic glial cells which have lost their intracranial connections and present as an exteacranial mass. Nasal glial heterotopia can be of three types – extranasal, intranasal, and mixed. The root of the nose is the most common location. They may masquerade as encephalocele or dermoid cyst.
Case Report: A 7-month-old who presented with a cystic mass at the bridge of the nose. He was diagnosed as nasal dermoid or nasal glioma on imaging. This mass was excised and sent for histopathological examination. It showed nasal glial heterotopia. Postoperative course was uneventful.
Case Discussion: This 7-month-old baby presented with a progressively increasing mass over the bridge of the nose since 3 months of age. Noncontrast computed tomography studies showed no evidence of intracranial communication of the lesion. The mass was excised, and on histopathological examination, it showed predominantly glial tissue with fibrillary neuroglial fibers interspersed by fibrovascular septae. Few astrocyte-like cells and ? Neurons in a fibrillary background and fibroconnective tissue. Focally cystocally dilated (? Entrapped nasal) glands lined by pseudo-stratified columnar epithelium. On immunohistochemistry using S100 and GFAP stains, glial fibrillary acidic protein was diffusely positive. Hence, the diagnosis of nasal glial heterotopia was made. The patient had an uneventful postoperative period.
Conclusion: Midline masses at the root of the nose are either nasal dermoid or anterior encephaloceles. Sometimes, dermoids have intracranial extension. Nasal glial hypertopia is a rare condition caused by the trapping of glial tissue during bony fusion occurring in the midline. The diagnosis is difficult to make. During surgery in this child, there was difficulty in excision at the upper part. However, there was no breech of dura. Imaging often not helpful in differentiating these lesions.
Nomogram of immature platelet fraction in pediatric patients in the Indian cohort: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
KrishnanNellaiGoelPrabudhSehgalTusharJainVisheshDhuaAnjanYadavDevendra KumarAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Background and Aims: Immature platelet fraction (IPF) can be a useful marker of thrombopoietic activity. It is a potential predictive marker for platelet recovery. However, the data available for normal values of IPF is limited to the adult population or in populations with a wide age range. This study aimed to identify the age-based IPF levels among children 1–14 years of age group and create a standard nomogram for the pediatric population in the Indian subcontinent.
Methodology: A targeted sample size of 100 children per group (each group spreading across 1 year) was calculated. The platelet count, IPF, mean platelet volume, and platelet distribution width were recorded for each child. Generalized additive models for location, scale, and shape were used. Non-parametric smoothing cubic spline functions of age with Box-Cox t distribution were adopted for model fitting. Based on the best-fitted model, the centiles curves of 3, 5, 10, 25, 50, 75, 90, 95, and 97 over age were calculated for IPF.
Results: The nomogram for IPF across the pediatric age-range is herewith generated and presented.
Approach for diagnosis and management of a child with ovotesticular disorder of sexual development: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
AShrilakshmiPathakManishDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: shriaithal96@gmail.com
Introduction: Ovotesticular disorder of sexual development (OT DSD) is a rare condition characterized by the histologic demonstration of ovarian and testicular tissue in the same individual. It has great genotype and phenotypic variability.
Case Details: A 4-year-old child, reared as a phenotypical male presented to our hospital to seek consultation for left undescended testis and abnormal appearing external genitalia. On examination, the patient was found to have well-developed phallus, with an opening at the junction of labioscrotal folds and phallus. The right testis was within the scrotum, soft testicular consistency, with normal rugosities of the scrotum, and left hemiscrotum was empty. Ultrasonography was suggestive of uterus-like structure posterior to the urinary bladder. Karyotyping was 46 XX, with normal serum electrolytes and hormone levels. On cystogenitoscopy, the patient was found to have a common channel of 2 cm length, with vaginal pouch and bladder neck opening at same level. The patient underwent diagnostic laparoscopy, where the left gonad was having dual consistency, with fallopian tube seen adjacent to the gonad and a small uterus. On the right side, vas deferenses were present. Both poles of the left gonad and right gonad were biopsied, which was found to be left ovotestis and right testis, and was hence diagnosed with OT DSD.
The patient is now being reared as phenotypical male, who will be planned for further management depending upon the child's decision of gender assignment.
Discussion: The diagnosis of OT DSD should be made if it has been histologically demonstrated that the ovary and testicular tissue coexist within the same gonad or in opposite gonads. A multidisciplinary approach should be employed in the management of these patients.
Desperate solutions for desperate problems (resecting vas, seminal vesicle, and epididymis): Category: MISCELLENEOUS
SinghGarvitaVermaMuniAggarwalSatish KumarDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: garvitasingh429@gmail.com
Introduction: We present a case of urethral duplication with previous multiple surgeries who presented to us with persistent left testicular pain and was evaluated and underwent resection of vas, seminal vesicle, and epididymis.
Case Report: A 14-year-old boy, initially diagnosed with sagittal Y type urethral duplication and bilateral undescended testes soon after birth, underwent first surgery at 11 months of age-correction of urethral duplication along with right scrotal orchidopexy. He then started having recurrent UTIs, poor flow, and left recurrent epididymitis. At 5 years, a cystoscopy revealed a urethral stricture requiring OIU and PUV which was ablated. At 6 years, he underwent left orchidopexy and epididymotomy. Left scrotal pain (epididymitis) persisted. A retrograde urethrogram had shown bilateral ureterovesical reflux. dye study
At our center, he was brought at 14 years with poor flow and recurrent and progressive left scrotal pain. Examination revealed a full bladder, hard left epididymis (upper pole testis). The evaluation revealed gross post-void residue with a large volume bladder, marginally raised creatinine, bilateral polycystic kidney disease, syringomyelia with multiple vertebral defects. Imaging also revealed bilateral seminal vesicle cysts/dilatation and vas dilatation and left testicular microcalcifications and shrunken right intraabdominal testis.
He then underwent laparoscopic excision of left vas and seminal vesicle along with trans-scrotal resection of vas and epididymis along with left testicular biopsy. Cystoscopic urethral dilatation was also done. Postoperatively, child recovered uneventfully, and his left testicular pain subsided. The right testis is intraabdominal and small. It will probably be removed at the time of a probable Mitrofanoff for bladder dysfunction. Urethral CIC is not feasible due to stricture and previous surgery for urethral duplication.
Conclusion: This is a rare case requiring the excision of all sperm transporting mechanism outside the testis, rendering the child azoospermic to treat him from the crippling left scrotal pain.
Congenital cervical atresia – A challenging rare anomaly and successful management: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
ShreyasKNayakShubhalaxmiPathakManishJadhavAvinashSinhaArvindDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: shreyas.m23@gmail.com
Introduction: Congenital atresia of the cervix is a rare Mullerian anomaly. Historically hysterectomy has been advocated as the management of choice. In recent years, conservative surgery is being recommended more frequently in patients with congenital cervical atresia and is shown to have a better reproductive performance. Here we report a case of cervical atresia successfully managed by surgical recanalization and cervicoplasty.
Case Report: A 13-year-old female with normal secondary sexual characteristics presented with complaints of cyclical abdominal pain of 2 months duration. Examination revealed female-type genitalia with normally developed labia majora, minora, and clitoris. Urethral and vaginal openings were identified with no obvious discharge from the vagina. ultrasonography pelvis was suggestive of a normal-sized uterus with a distended endometrial cavity and a bulky cervix. The endocervical canal was distended with internal echogenic content without vascularity. Bilateral ovaries were normal. MR pelvis was suggestive of the distended cervix and uterine cavity suggestive of hematocervicometra and a normal vagina. The possibility of a transverse vaginal septum was suggested.
Genitoscopy was suggestive of a normal vagina and the fornices with nonvisualization of the cervical os. However, a prominent bulge was noted in the posterior wall of the vagina with a small dimpling. Upon inserting a needle, menstrual blood could be aspirated, and the diagnosis of cervical atresia was made. She underwent surgical recanalization of the cervix with cervicoplasty. In follow-up, the child is asymptomatic and is due for her menstruation. Cervical dilatation is planned after the first menses.
Conclusion: Surgical canalization in selected patients with congenital cervical atresia can be successfully performed to provide patients an opportunity for conservative management, resulting in normal menstrual bleeding, resolution of cyclic pelvic pain, and some potential for fertility.
Aggressive multimodality treatment of cervicofacial hemangioma with Kasabach–Merritt syndrome: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
VinodVenkata SaiVarshaShrishtiVinayAcharyaSamir KantPuriArchanaJhadavAmitSugandhiNidhiDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, VMMC and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: venkatasaivinod@gmail.com
Introduction: Kasabach–Merritt syndrome is a triad comprising of rapidly growing vascular tumor, consumptive coagulopathy, and thrombocytopenia-which presents as a life-threatening condition. Recent advances have shown that an aggressive multimodality approach can ensure complete recovery.
Case Report: A 1-year-old male child was brought with swelling in the right side of face for 6 months with a rapid increase in size over 4 days. The swelling was of 15 × 10 cm size involving the right side of the face and neck and extending intraorally. The child was diagnosed clinically with cervicofacial hemangioma. A ultrasonography Doppler done confirmed the diagnosis. The child was started on tablet propanolol. Thereafter, the swelling showed rapid enlargement causing respiratory distress, thrombocytopenia, and bleeding diathesis. The child was then started on Inj Hydrocortisone and received multiple platelet transfusions but with no response. The child underwent an emergency tracheostomy for persistent respiratory distress and stridor. The child post-surgically was started on injection methylprednisolone as well as weekly injection vincristine. The child showed decrease in swelling size. Platelet count normalized in 1 week. The child was successfully discharged on tracheostomy 1 month after admission on oral steroids and T. Propanolol and was reviewed weekly for injcetion Vincristine. Magnetic resonance imaging done 4 months after discharge showed a significant reduction in size.
Discussion: Kasabach–Merritt Syndrome is life-threatening complication associated with vascular tumors of childhood. It results in consumptive coagulopathy causing uncontrollable bleeding diathesis and death; prompt diagnosis and initiation of therapy are critical in Kasabach–Merritt phenomenon.
Lower extremity fibro-adipose vascular anomaly: A new and rare case of lower extremity overgrowth: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
KalagotlaChandrikaAggarwalSatish KumarNandanBrijeshVarmaMuniSinghGarvitaDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India

Introduction: A cohort of patients with a unique intramuscular lesion of the extremities comprising dense fibrofatty tissue and slow-flow vascular malformations have been recently identified. The acronym Fibro-Adipose Vascular Anomaly (FAVA) has been recently given to a distinct vascular entity that is characterized by fibrofatty infiltration of muscle, unusual phlebectasia with pain, and contracture of the affected extremity. We report a case of FAVA in a 16-month-old girl with pain in her right lower leg. As in our case, FAVA typically presents in young females with calf involvement. FAVA should be considered a differential diagnosis when evaluating vascular anomalies in the lower extremities.
Case Report: A 16-month-old girl was referred to us for a mass in the right calf which was noticed by the parents during routine care. Upon examination at our institution, there was a visible, nontender mass in the lateral aspect of the right lower leg. No skin findings were observed. We obtained radiographs, right calf mass ultrasound, and lower extremities magnetic resonance imaging which showed a 3 cm × 4 cm complex mass in the right calf consistent with a FAVA involving calf muscles. A complete excision of mass and tenodesis of peroneus brevis to periosteum was done. The child recovered completely. At 1-year follow-up, there is no recurrence and no limb deformity or functional loss
Conclusion: Many of such lesions are erroneously diagnosed as hemangiomas or venolymphatic malformations and may be subjected to beta-blockers or sclerotherapy.

Tamarind seeds in tracheobronchial tree: Expect complications: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
RevanthKallaBorkarNitinkumarSharmaCharuKumarAshwinDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Raipur, Chhattisgarh, India
E-mail: revanthkalla94@gmail.com
Aim: Foreign body aspiration is very common among the childhood population and can cause significant morbidity and mortality. Among the various foreign bodies aspirated in the form of vegetable seeds, tamarind seeds are uncommon. Due to the size of tamarind seed and subsequent increase in size after aspiration into the tracheobronchial tree, it becomes challenge for retrieval of it.
Methods: We with this article like to present a series of four cases with aspirated tamarind seeds and the challenges encountered during their bronchoscopic removal.
Results: Two patients needed tracheotomy for removal of the tamarind as it was not possible to remove it through the narrowed glottis. In other two patients, the foreign body was removed piecemeal after crushing from the bronchus itself as it has formed there a pseudo-cavitation and was not moving from the impacted area.
Conclusion: Removal of aspirated tamarind seeds from the tracheobronchial tree is challenging. Anticipating the difficulty and being prepared well will reduce intraoperative difficulty and allow successful removal with favorable patient outcomes.
Genetic gonadal discordance: 46XX, SRY negative ovotesticular disorder of sexual differentiation: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
SaravagolVidya MNMamathaAnandADepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Bangalore Medical College and Research institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India
E-mail: vidya095@gmail.com
Background: SRY-negative 46 XX ovotesticular disorder of sexual differentiation (DSD) represents a very rare and unique DSD condition where testicular tissue develops in the absence of Y chromosome.
Case Presentation: A 11-month-old child reared as male was brought to our outpatient department with a history of ambiguous genitalia since birth and abnormal urinary orifice noticed by the mother. There was no history of any acute illness and emergency admission in past. On examination, the child has a phallus length of 2 cm, meatus opening at junction of phallus and labioscrotal folds, gonads not palpable. Blood investigations 17-OH progesterone, electrolytes and testosterone and dihydroxy testosterone were normal. On HCG stimulation, there was a significant increase of testosterone and DHT level. Genitocystoscopy showed normal urethra, no impressions of prostatic utricle/cervix seen, and bladder normal. Diagnostic laparoscopy both gonads was noted near deep inguinal ring, biopsy was done. No Mullerian structure was found. Biopsy showed left streak gonad and right Ovo testis. Since the child was reared as male and parental preference to continue the same and also there was a significant increase in Testosterone and DHT post-HCG, the child underwent bilateral gonadopexy to scrotum. FISH done – 46XX with absent SRY gene. AMH levels awaited.
Conclusion: SRY negative 46XX Ovotestis is rare form of DSD. For ovotesticular DSD with 46XX, female gender is suggested as the best sex of rearing. However, in our case, the patient was already being reared as male, evaluation showing absent uterus with normal testosterone and DHT levels which significantly increased after HCG stimulation; in addition, parental preference was to continue the gender of rearing as male. Cytogenetics, molecular analysis, type of gonad, hormonal assay, and presence or absence of Mullerian structures all contribute to the gender of rearing.
Management of perineal canal: Category: MISCELLENEOUS
SahuShyam SundarBhartiyaSubhash ChandraSinghAbhishek KumarRanjanAbhishekBiruaHirendraDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Rajendra Institute of Medical Sciences, Ranchi, Jharkhand, India
E-mail: dr_shyamsundarsahu@yahoo.co.in
Aims: This study aims to evaluate the outcome of perineal canal repair with modified Tsuchida's technique in our setup.
Methods: From June 2018 to June 2022, consecutively treated patients with perineal canal were included in this study.
Results: A total of 8 cases of perineal canal were included in the study. In the first three cases, modified Tsuchida's technique was used for perineal canal repair with covering colostomy. In the next two cases, modified Tsuchida's technique was done without covering colostomy, of which one case had severe subcutaneous infection, and diversion colostomy had to be done. The other case had a mild secondary infection of wound. So afterward, we had sought for the technique with covering colostomy always. Colostomy closure was done after 3 months.
Conclusion: Treatment of perineal canal is excision of the fistula with anal mucosal cuff as described by Tsuchida. Covering colostomy should be done for the healing of the perineal inflammation. Modified Tsuchida's technique of perineal repair has shown good results in our study.

Aims: This study aims to study the clinicomicrobiological profile of newborn breast abscesses treated at a tertiary center in Nepal.
Methods: A retrospective review of neonates presenting with breast abscesses between 2021 and 2022 was done. Their clinical profile, management outline and microbiological findings and follow-up details were studied.
Results: Two neonates (both boys) were identified with breast abscesses at 13 and 15 days of life, respectively. The first case had a fluctuant swelling over the left breast that was confirmed by aspiration, while the second case had an indurated swelling over the right breast with ultrasonogram showing a collection. Total colonic aganglionosis with anorectal malformation: A rare association: Category: NEONATAL
MenghwaniHimanshuPiplaniRajatYhoshuEnonoGuptaManish KumarAhmadIntezarBalijaSatya SreeDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, Rishikesh, Uttarakhand, India
E-mail: himanshumegwani96@gmail.com
Background: The association between Hirschsprung's disease (HD) and anorectal malformation (ARM) is uncommon. However, the exact incidence of this rare occurrence is not known, but it has been reported in 2.3%–3.4% of ARM cases. To the best of our knowledge, not a single case of total colonic aganglionosis (TCA) with ARM has been reported in the literature. We hereby report a rare association in a neonate presented with a perineal fistula with gross abdominal distension not responding to rectal washes and dilatation.
Case Report: A 3-day-old female child, with 2.5 kg weight, presented with complaints of nonpassage of stools since birth associated with progressive abdominal distension. On examination, the abdomen was tense and distended along with low ARM (perineal fistula). The patient was started on rectal washes and progressive dilatation of the fistulous opening. The patient did not respond to the conservative approach and hence a high-divided sigmoid colostomy was planned on Day 5. Intraoperatively, sigmoid colon and rectum were found to be filled with thick meconium, with a normal caliber sigmoid colon. Thorough proximal and distal bowel washes were given in the postoperative period but the stoma did not function. On Day 8, retrograde contrast study through the stoma showed an area of narrowing in the distal ileum. On re-exploration, a transition zone was found near the ICJ of about 5 cm following which a divided ileostomy was performed. Biopsies from distal ileum, transverse colon, and sigmoid colon revealed absent ganglion cells and nerve bundle hypertrophy suggestive of total colonic aganglionosis. At discharge, the child was tolerating feeds well and passing stool through stoma, and has also gained weight on follow-up at 1 month.
Conclusion: The simultaneous occurrence of HD and anorectal malformation is an extremely rare condition. The association of TCA and ARM is even rarer. TCA is difficult to diagnose in neonates with low ARM preoperatively as in both conditions patient presents with difficulty or nonpassage of meconium at birth. A case of low ARM with fistula with persistent abdominal distension not responding to rectal washes and dilatation should raise a suspicion of HD and should be subjected to contrast enema to rule out this rare association.
