SinghUmesh BahadurGargDileepMathurVinayGroverJitendraDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Gajra Raja Medical College, Gwalior, Madhya Pradesh, India
E-mail: singhub.2008@gmail.com
Aims: Congenital pouch colon (CPC) is a very rare abnormality in which varying degrees of pouch-like dilatation of dysplastic colon is associated with an anorectal malformation. The pouch usually communicates with a fistulous track with the genitourinary tract. CPC is classified on anatomic morphology into five types as per the length of the colon involved. In “Saxena and mathur Classification based on anatomic morphology the type 5 CPC shows a double ouch colon with short normal interpositioned colon segment. CPC malformation is exclusively seen in Northern India, only 7.8% of cases of (CPC) have been reported outside India. However, recently there is increasing awareness about this rare anomaly and some cases are now reported from other parts of the world. Aims of this case series were to evaluate the incidence of duplication of appendix associated with CPC in cases of ARM.
Methods: We report three cases of duplication of appendix associated with type I CPC out of 25 cases of pouch colon encountered in the last 3 year duration. The medical record has been extracted and reviewed from all case records of cases of ARM managed in the last 3 years.
Results: In all cases, appendectomy was done along with bladder fistula ligation and pouchorrhaphy. All cases awaiting definitive repair for anorectal malformation at present.
Conclusion: Congenital abnormalities of the appendix are rare and duplication of the appendix is an extremely rare condition, with a reported incidence of 0.004% of appendectomy specimens and found as an incidental finding during surgery.
Limb preservation in extremity soft-tissue tumors: A desirable and feasible goal: Category: ONCOLOGY
KanodiaShrishtiTotadriVarsha MVinodVenkatasaiShelkeVinayakumarJadhavAmitAcharyaSamir KPuriArchanaSugandhiNidhiDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Vardhaman Mahavir Medical College and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: SHRISHTI008@GMAIL.COM
Introduction: Historically, amputation has been treatment for large tumors involving neurovascular bundles in the extremities. Recently, there is a changing trend to limb-sparing excision.
Case Details: A 10-year-old male presented with a gradually progressive swelling in the right popliteal fossa for the past 6 months. Examination revealed 12 cm × 10 cm hard fixed swelling within the popliteal fossa extending to the thigh. Magnetic resonance imaging demonstrated large mass in the popliteal fossa and thigh, completely encasing the popliteal vessels and sciatic nerve. Tru cut Biopsy divulged a nonspecific mesenchymal tumor. Suspecting low-grade Rhabdomyosarcoma chemotherapy was initiated but showed no response and, a surgical excision was planned. A multidisciplinary team including pediatric surgery, plastic surgery, and vascular surgery undertook the excision and reconstruction. Intraoperatively Mass involving biceps femoris, semi-membranous, semi-tendinosis, and gastrocnemius was removed along with a 12 cm segment of popliteal vessels coursing through the mass reconstruction of artery and vein was done with great saphenous venous graft from contralateral limb. Sciatic nerve could be preserved. Child is on regular follow-up with mild foot drop improving with rehabilitation. Histopathologically, the excised tissue had features suggestive of fibromatosis with clear margins.
Conclusion: Limb preservation is possible even in large tumors encasing neurovascular bundles with meticulous planning and multidisciplinary approach. In the pediatric population, limb preservation should be aimed for considering the long-term functional and cosmetic needs.
Keywords: Extremities, limb preservation, soft tissue tumors
Ossifying renal tumor of infancy: Category: ONCOLOGY
BanerjeeArkaReddyK AbhishekMunghateGayatriBendrePradnya SBodhanwalaMinnieDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, B J Wadia Hospital for Children, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: arkabanerjee6989@gmail.com
Introduction: An extremely rare pediatric renal tumor, ossifying renal tumor of infancy (ORTI) usually presents in male infants with intermittent episodes of painless gross hematuria. The tumor is benign and surgically treatable. Imaging typically shows a preserved renal outline with a calcified intrapelvic mass. Microscopically, these lesion shows varying components of osteoid, osteoblastic cells, and spindle cells. However, the etiology is unclear.
We report a case of ORTI and review the literature to assess the clinicopathological features of this unusual neoplasm.
Case Report: A 5-month-old boy was admitted with isolated gross hematuria. Ultrasound showed a well-defined solid mass in the lower pole of the right kidney that was heterogenous on Computed Tomography (CT) but without calcifications. Nephrectomy was done and histopathology showed an ossified core surrounded by a spindle cell component confirming the diagnosis of an ORTI.
Discussion: ORTIs are extremely rare. First reported in 1980, there are only 29 published cases in the English literature. The median age at diagnosis is 4.25 months. ORTIs are 6 times more common in males. Almost all patients have gross hematuria at presentation. It appears as a homogeneous, hyperechoic renal mass with posterior acoustic shadowing on ultrasound and an intrapelvic calcified renal mass with poor postcontrast enhancement on CT. T2-hypointensity on Magnetic resonance imaging is a unique feature of ORTI. Surgical excision is curative and recurrence or malignant disease is unheard of. Histologically, ORTIs are characterized by varying components of osteoid, osteoblastic cells, and spindle cells. Clonal trisomy 4 is a characteristic finding on florescent in situ hybridization-probing.
Conclusion: ORTI is a rare benign pediatric renal tumor with some typical clinical, radiological and pathological features. A male infant with painless intermittent macroscopic hematuria with an endophytic calcified renal mass involving the calyceal system points towards an ORTI as the possible diagnosis.
Rare childhood retroperitoneal tumors: An experience from a tertiary center in nepal: Category: ONCOLOGY
ShresthaAshish LalDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Ashish Lal Shrestha/Kathmandu Medical College and Teaching Hospital, Sinamangal, Kathmandu, Nepal
E-mail: ashishlalshrestha75@gmail.com
Aims: To review the pattern of uncommon retroperitoneal tumors in Nepalese children, their clinical profile, management outcomes, and follow-up data.
Methods: A retrospective review of retroperitoneal solid tumors in children treated between 2017 and 2022 was done. Demographic profile, investigative details, and management parameters were recorded. Standard management and follow-up protocols were followed for individual cases.
Results: Of 4 children, 2 had undergone radical nephroureterectomy with lymph node sampling for Clear Cell Sarcoma Kidney-1 and Suprarenal Neuroblastoma involving the entire kidney-1. Of these, the former was diagnosed on preoperative biopsy from the flank while the latter was diagnosed as Chemo refractory Wilms’ tumor without prior biopsy. The 3rd case was diagnosed to be Castleman's disease on histopathology of the gross total excision specimen of retroperitoneal mass although preoperative biopsy suggested a mesenchymal tumor. Moreover, the 4th case was diagnosed as Stage 4S Neuroblastoma that was managed with close follow-ups. All the children survived without immediate or long-term morbidities.
Conclusion: Preoperative tissue sampling can facilitate the diagnosis and treatment of rare childhood retroperitoneal tumors while management following standard protocols can result in favorable outcomes.

Giant retroperitoneal teratoma in an infant: A surgical challenge: Category: ONCOLOGY
JhaVimalenduJainVisheshDhuaAnjanGoelPrabudhYadavD KDevasenathipathyKDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, New Delhi, India
E-mail: docvjha@gmail.com
Background: Retroperitoneal teratomas (RPT) are uncommon tumors in children and comprise 3.5 − 4% of all germ cell tumors. They contain variable degrees of mature, immature, and frankly malignant elements, most commonly foci of yolk sac tumor. The common sites include sacrococcygeal region, gonads, mediastinum, and retroperitoneum. Surgery is challenging because of the large size, close proximity to blood vessels, and involvement of the retroperitoneal organs.
Case Presentation: A 6-month-old girl presented with rapidly progressing abdominal distension of 3 months’ duration without any associated symptoms. A 10 × 8 cm, firm to hard, nontender, smooth surfaced globular lump occupying all the quadrants of the abdomen was present. Laboratory investigations including AFP levels were within normal range. contrast-enhanced computed tomography showed a solid-cystic well-defined lesion in the retroperitoneum with areas of calcification and fat necrosis, lifting the aorta and IVC, displacing right kidney and bowel loops. Laparotomy revealed a 10 cm × 12 cm large, solid-cystic, well-encapsulated retroperitoneal mass occupying almost the entire abdomen, pushing bowel loops laterally and right kidney superiorly with right renal vessels and ureter stretched over the mass. It was passing behind the IVC displacing the aorta to the left. The mass was excised with meticulous dissection while preserving all the vital structures. Postoperative recovery was uneventful. Histopathology reported features of mature teratoma with the presence of ectodermal, mesodermal, and endodermal components.
Conclusion: The diagnosis of RPT is based on clinical suspicion and imaging. Complete surgical resection is generally curative. The adequacy of surgical resection depends on the ability to dissect the tumor from the vital retroperitoneal structures. The prognosis is generally good if the tumor is completely removed. Histopathological examination confirms the presence of a teratoma. The presence of malignant elements on pathologic analysis requires close clinical follow-up and consideration of chemotherapy in unlikely case of a recurrence.
A pediatric case of metachronous pheochromocytoma: Category: ONCOLOGY
AchugatlaSushmaSharmaRahul DeoSinghSurendraRedkarRajeevLilavati Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: Sushmaachugatla10@gmail.com
Background: Pheochromocytoma (PCCs) are rare chromaffin cell tumors which secrete catecholamines and form part of neuroendocrine tumors. PCCs occur sporadically, as well as they are inherited in combination with syndromes. In this paper, we highlight the onset of metachronous origin in an operated case of PCC in an adolescent girl, who is found to have VHL gene mutation on the genetic screening done later.
Case Report: A 15-year-old girl presented with weakness and profuse sweating for 1 week. She had a history of a right-sided adrenal mass which was pheochromocytoma excised in 2016, and she was on anti-hypertensive since then. On clinical examination, she was tall and moderate built with no skin lesions. Blood and urine investigations were done which showed raised plasma and urinary nor-metanephrine. DOTA PET scan showed 3 cm × 2.6 cm × 4.4 cm sized lobulated lesion in the left-sided adrenal mass with high-grade somatostatin receptor expression suggestive of left-sided pheochromocytoma. Laparoscopic left-sided cortical sparing tumor removal was performed. The patient was discharged on cortisol supplements. Histopathology was revealed as pheochromocytoma. A gross decrease in plasma normetanephrines was found after a week. Genetic screening of the patient reported VHL gene mutation with Autosomal dominant inheritance. Magnetic resonance imaging brain and plasma normetanephrines are advised to repeat after 2 months.
Conclusion: Since the inheritance of these tumors with new gene mutations is increasing and carries a high rate of recurrence and malignancy, genetic screening is recommended in all patients with pheochromocytoma and lifelong follow-up for recurrent tumors is mandatory.
A curious case of bilateral wilms’ tumor: Complexities galore: Category: ONCOLOGY
SehgalMehakJainVisheshYadavDevendraDhuaAnjanGoelPrabudhJanaManishaKandasamyDevasenathipathyDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: mehak.sehgal91@gmail.com
Introduction: Bilateral Wilms’ tumor is a rare entity with its associated surgical challenges. However, the scenario in our patient was further complicated by renal vein and IVC thrombus, along with acute anthracycline toxicity.
Case Report: A 1.5-year-old girl presented with gradually progressive abdominal distention and constipation. Computed tomography (CT) showed bilateral renal masses and she was started on DD4A chemotherapy. Due to poor response, renal biopsy was performed at 10 weeks and chemotherapy continued till 12 weeks. Biopsy was suggestive of Wilms’ tumor with rhabdomyoblastic differentiation. She also developed facial puffiness and hypertension. A preoperative echocardiogram confirmed acute anthracycline toxicity with severe left ventricular dysfunction which needed anti-hypertensives, diuretics, along with an anti-coagulation for a left ventricular clot, that created additional anesthetic challenges. Before resection at 12 weeks, CT was suggestive of bilateral renal masses with renal vein thrombus on the right side. Intraoperatively however, there was a large vena-cava thrombus extending from duplicated right renal veins, which had to be resected by cavotomy. A partial nephrectomy was performed on the left side and a double-J stent was placed. Postoperatively, the child had adequate renal function but developed a peri-nephric urine leak, which gradually subsided. The child was discharged in stable condition and is doing well at follow-up.
Conclusion: Apart from the technical challenges of surgical resection in bilateral Wilms’ tumor, our case was further complicated by anthracycline toxicity as well as IVC thrombus, which could not be detected despite extensive preoperative workup. A systematic approach and thorough preoperative work-up facilitated a good surgical outcome despite the multiple challenges.
Atrophic kidney-like lesion: A rare albeit important differential for renal tumors – A case report: Category: ONCOLOGY
BhattSomyaPathakManishNayakShubhalaxmiSinhaArvindDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: somya.3bhatt@gmail.com
Introduction: Originally described by Hes et al., atrophic kidney-like lesion (AKLL) is a provisional renal entity with distinct morphological and immunohistochemical features. It characteristically resembles thyroid-like follicular renal cell carcinoma of the kidney. Very few cases of AKLL have been reported in the literature. Despite a paucity of cases and limited follow-up data, it is considered to be a benign renal neoplasm with indolent behavior. The rarity of these lesions and their typical incidental discovery will make it a diagnostic challenge.
Case: We hereby describe the clinicopathological features of AKLL in a 10-year-old female. The patient presented with complaints of pain on the right side of abdomen on and off for one year. Abdominal examination findings were unremarkable with no palpable mass. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography abdomen showed a well-defined exophytic hypodense mass lesion, measuring 2.4 cm × 2.7 cm × 3.4 cm, arising from the lower pole of the right kidney extending till renal pelvis. There were peripheral foci of calcification and no apparent renal vascular invasion seen. Hence, the patient underwent right nephroureterectomy. HPE revealed an encapsulated cellular tumor with atrophic round tubules admixed with cystically dilated tubules and calcifications. On IHC, diffuse positivity for PAX-8 suggestive of AKLL was noted. Currently, the patient has been asymptomatic for 6 months in follow-up.
Conclusion: AKLL is a diagnostic challenge, especially in the pediatric population where other renal tumors are more prevalent. Radiologically, as well it is difficult to differentiate AKLL because of the lack of any specific characteristics. Pathological examination is diagnostic for this entity. The present case had a well-defined capsule, occasional amorphous calcification with PAX8 positivity. This case thus emphasizes the fact that preoperative diagnosis of AKLL is difficult and histopathological examination can help clinch the diagnosis of this rare entity.
Synchronous Wilms’ tumor in the right kidney and right thorax: Successful management: Category: ONCOLOGY
VermaVarshaAggarwalSatish KumarKalraManasVarmaMuniSinghGarvitaSachdevaDivijDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: varsha_verma18@yahoo.com
Background: Wilms’ tumor/nephroblastoma is the most commonly identified renal mass in children <5 years, synchronuous involvement of thoracic lesion with similarly favorable histology is rare.
Case Report: A 2-year-old presented with right renal mass. On investigation, he was found to have a right renal mass characteristics of Wilms’ tumor. In addition, there was a large right thoracic mass. Biopsy from the thoracic mass showed Wilms’ tumor (favorable histology). He received 6 cycles of preoperative chemotherapy. The size of kidney mass reduced and right radical nephrectomy was done. At surgery, diaphragm was intact with a contiguous lesion in chest. HPE showed triphasic Wilms’ tumor with undiffrentiated blastemal cells. All lymph nodes are negative. After 4 more cycles of chemotherapy, he was evaluated with Angio computed tomography (CT) chest-CT revealed a complex mass with lobulations and extension into the oblique and transverse fissure with possible adherence/involvement of the right lower lung lobe. The lesion was found to be going along the inferior pulmonary vein, trying to encase aorta >180 ° with subcarinal extension. He underwent right thoracotomy and resection of thoracic mass. It was adherent to the diaphragm and lower lung lobe, part of which was removed with stapler. HPE was the same as kidney mass.
Discussion: Synchronous occurrence of Wilms’ tumor in kidney and extrarenal site is extremely rare and contiguous spread from kidney to supradiaphragmatic area may be thought first. In this case, since the diaphragm was intact, contiguous spread was ruled out. Literature review will be presented.
B/L Wilms’ tumor-poor response to chemotherapy: Case report: Category: ONCOLOGY
KalagotlaChandrikaAggarwalSatish KumarKalraManasAggarwalGarvita SinghAyushDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: drkchandrika2k9@gmail.com
Introduction: Wilms’ tumor is the most common malignant renal tumor in the pediatric age group and is classically managed by multimodal treatment which involves surgery, radiotherapy, and chemotherapy. The majority of Wilms’ tumor (WT) responds well to preoperative chemotherapy. B/L Wilms’ tumor is managed by preoperative chemotherapy followed by nephron-sparing surgery at least on one side. Usual histology is favorable and there is good response to chemotherapy. We present a case of very large B/L Wilms’ tumor in an infant, whose tumor was nonresponsive to usual and advanced chemotherapy. Technical details of surgery and challenges we faced because of large B/L tumors will be discussed.
Case Report: We present on a case of B/L very large Wilms’ tumor with favorable histology which had poor response to usual and advanced chemotherapy. Following advanced chemotherapy, she had undergone left nephron-sparing surgery (NSS). The patient is waiting for the right NSS.
Discussion: We present a case of very large B/L Wilms’ tumor in an infant, whose tumor was nonresponsive to usual and advanced chemotherapy. Because of poor response, a biopsy was taken which showed favorable histology. Usual chemotherapy was given with 6 cycles of Vincristine, Actinomycin-D, and Doxorubicin (VAD regimen). Advanced chemotherapy was given with 6 cycles of VAD regimen and cyclophosphamide. After reviewing computed tomography (CT) angio, left NSS was done with successful preservation of lower pole of the kidney. The technical details and challenges faced because of B/L very large tumors will be presented and discussed. The histology report for this case showed favorable histology with more than 33% viable tumor having stromal and epithelial components, no focal anaplasia. The patient was waiting for right nephron sparing surgery, which may be difficult as there is multifocal involvement.
Conclusion: Stromal component in Wilms’ tumor may lead to the enhancement of growth and maturation induced by chemotherapy. This might be the possible reason for the poor response of WT in this case.
Wilms’ tumor with metastasis to contralateral suprarenal region: A rare presentation: Category: ONCOLOGY
BhagatDevprabhaPathakManishDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: devprabhabhagat@gmail.com
Methods: We describe a case of 2-year-old male child who presented with complaints of abdominal pain and distension for 7 months. He was evaluated but the patient defaulted treatment and presented after 6 months with worsening abdominal distension and respiratory distress. Initially, imaging showed a 9.1 cm × 7.6 cm × 6.7 cm mass arising from inferior pole of the right kidney which progressed to 20 cm × 13 cm × 15.7 cm also involving mid-kidney with a 5.9 cm × 4.6 cm × 3.7 cm lesion in the left suprarenal region on second presentation. The tumor was responsive to neoadjuvant chemotherapy. Right radical nephrectomy with left suprarenal metastatectomy was done for the child followed by abdominal radiation and DD4A regime. The child developed postradiotherapy adhesive intestinal obstruction after 1 month for which he underwent adhesiolysis. After completing DD4A, repeat imaging showed no residual tumor/recurrence with significant clinical improvement of the child.
Results: Wilms’ tumor has the best prognosis amongst childhood tumors; delayed presentation may lead to spread of the tumor which could result in poorer outcome and higher complication rates. In our case, there was a suspicion of left adrenal gland metastasis on imaging. Histopathology report, however, confirmed that no adrenal gland was present in the resected specimen; only contained metastatic deposit with epithelial, stromal, and blastemal component similar to the primary tumor.
Conclusion: Our case highlights that upon following the proper protocol of management including surgery with adjuvant and neoadjuvant chemotherapy and radiotherapy, a good clinical outcome can still be achieved despite such a rare presentation of suprarenal metastasis.
A rare case of a mediastinal fetus-in-fetu: Category: ONCOLOGY
BhagatDevprabhaPathakManishNayakShubhalaxmiDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: devprabhabhagat@gmail.com
Methods: We describe a case of 12-year-old adolescent male who presented to us with complaints of right-sided chest pain and intermittent breathlessness for the past 5 months. Clinical examination revealed reduced air entry and chest movement on the right side. A plain radiograph showed the presence of a silhouette sign over the right heart border. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography thorax showed a heterogeneous mass of 9.6 cm × 10.1 cm × 11 cm of mediastinum extending into the right hemithorax-fatty with loculated cysts, calcifications with underdeveloped limbs and ill-developed vertebral column and mass effect suggestive of fetus-in-fetu with loss of fat planes to surrounding structures. Intraoperatively, the tumor was found densely adhered to SVC, thymus, lung, and pericardial border but could be dissected away carefully from adjacent structures without any need for lobectomy or damage to vital structures. The phrenic nerve was dissected away after incising the mediastinal pleura and the tumor was removed in toto. Postoperatively, the child had a good clinical outcome.
Discussion: Fetus-in-fetu is a rare condition defined by the presence of axial skeleton and organoid formation with an incidence around 1 in 500,000 and only 5 intrathoracic cases have been reported so far. The presence of notochord characteristically differentiates it from a teratoma. The surgical approach in our case involved the in-toto excision while maintaining meticulous hemostasis and adequate clearance of involved surrounding structures with special emphasis to preserve the phrenic nerve. However, major vascular reconstruction may be needed but safe resection is feasible with careful dissection.

Background/Aim: Small round blue cell tumor arising from suprarenal gland is a rare, exceptional and highly malignant entity with very few cases reported in literature.
Case: We present a case of 9-year-old girl with right-sided abdominal lump which presented to us with pain over the right flank. After proper investigations, the abdominal mass was surgically resected and sent for histopathological and cytogenetic examination. Rare case of mature teratoma masquerading as intrathoracic venolymphatic lesion in a newborn: Category: THORACIC
BhaskarNirmalNarayananSarath KumarBineshAHridyaM KDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Government Medical College, Kozhikode, Kerala, India
E-mail: nirmalbhaskar321@gmail.com
Background and Aim: To present a rare case of intrathoracic lesion in a 4-month-old baby who presented with respiratory distress.
Case Presentation: A 4-month-old baby presented with respiratory distress since birth which progressed in severity to reach oxygen dependence. Imaging confirmed a large intrathoracic venolymphatic malformation that occupied the entire left hemithorax abutting pericardium and pulmonary vessels and leading to collapse of left lobes of lung, mediastinal shift to right and compression of the right lung. The baby was initially stabilized and then taken up for surgery. As the baby was too sick due to intrathoracic mass effect, cystic areas were drained using a pigtail catheter. When the situation improved, left posterolateral thoracotomy was done. Tumour was dissected off from pericardium and pulmonary vessels and removed in toto. The baby was extubated soon after operation. The patient was discharged after 7 days and is now being followed up. Postoperative histopathological examination confirmed it as a mature teratoma.
Conclusion: Mature teratoma presenting as intrathoracic lymphovenous malformation in a newborn is an extremely rare condition. We have demonstrated that life-threatening intrathoracic lymphovenous malformations can be treated initially by drainage followed by thoracotomy excision.
Stomach shadow in thorax: Diagnostic dilemma in two cases: Category: THORACIC
BanerjeeRupaVizPoonam GuhaBasuKalyaniDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Nil Ratna Sircar Medical College and Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India
E-mail: dctrrupa@gmail.com
Aim: We report two cases of acute respiratory distress with stomach shadow in the thorax.
Case 1: Three years old boy presented with chief complaint of acute respiratory distress after he fell down from bed. On examination he was tachypneic and his Spo2 was 92%. He was started on oxygen; a size #12 NG was passed and after stabilization chest X-ray was done. X-ray showed stomach gas shadow in left hemithorax with NG in situ and diagnosis of? Eventration of diaphragm or? Tension gastrothorax was made. Emergency surgery was planned. He underwent right subcostal incision with reduction of stomach and bowel loops in abdomen and repair of ruptured diaphragm. Postoperative course was smooth and he was discharged in stable condition. On 5 months’ follow–up, he is doing well.
Case 2: An 8-month-old girl presented to emergency with complaints of acute respiratory distress, nonbilious vomiting, reduced urine output, and features of shock. Emergency management was done. X-ray abdomen showed a single gas shadow. Upper g. i contrast showed dilated lower esophagus and dye reaching stomach but not beyond. Emergency laparotomy was performed which reveled gastric volvulus with organo-axial rotation and areas of necrosis, further Ladds bands were seen and Ladds procedure with gastrostomy was done. Post-operative she pass stool and feeding was established via gastrostomy but she had respiratory distress. Chest X-ray showed opacity in left lower lung and she was started on physiotherapy and nebulization. She had complaints of gastrostomy leak and dye study was done through tube and the leak was managed. POD 20 child still required oxygen support and had respiratory distress. All these factors lead to not establishment of oral feeds and hence to rule out any issues with gastrostomy per oral dye study was done and then a small bowel loop was seen in the left hemithorax. Diagnosis of left CDH was made and the child was taken up for surgery and repair done along with partial gastrectomy and FJ was done. Rest of the post-operative course was smooth. She was discharged with FJ feeds and small liquid feeds orally. On 4 months’ follow–up, she is doing well and is gaining weight slowly.
Conclusion: Gastric volvulus is mostly associated with diaphragmatic hernia, eventration, or rarely tension gastrothorax. Increased awareness of this condition and association, along with careful history and examination, leads to accurate diagnosis and correct management
Incidental finding of tracheal duplication cyst in a neonate with tracheoesophageal fistula: Category: THORACIC
AShrilakshmiJadhavAvinashNayakShubhalaxmiDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: shriaithal96@gmail.com
Duplication of the trachea is a rare anomaly, with <5 cases reported in the literature. Presentation of tracheal duplication can be ambiguous; the patient may remain asymptomatic till adulthood or present early with varied symptoms. No proper guidelines have been established regarding the management of tracheal duplication cyst.
Case Details: A full-term, male neonate was brought at 44 h of life with complaints of excessive drooling of saliva and intolerance to oral feeds noticed since birth. On clinical examination, the patient had respiratory distress with tachyapnea and intercostal retraction. A 10Fr feeding tube was negotiable till 9–10 cm in the esophagus after which coiling of the tube was present. After chest X-ray, the patient was diagnosed as esophageal atresia with trachea-esophageal fistula. The patient underwent right postero-lateral thoracotomy and tracheoesophageal fistula repair on day 3 of life. Intraoperatively, the patient had Type C tracheoesophageal fistula with proximal pouch at level of T1. Primary anastomosis of the esophagus was achieved. A thick-walled mucus-containing cystic lesion measuring 1 cm × 0.5 cm × 0.2 cm, was identified communicating with the right side of trachea proximal to the bifurcation. The cyst was excised and the site of tracheal communication was repaired using vicryl 5-0 suture material. Histopathology of the cyst showed flattened tissue lined by respiratory lining epithelium. The sub epithelium showed seromucinous glands, cartilage, fibrous tissue, and few congested blood vessels, suggestive of tracheal duplication cyst.
Discussion: Due to rarity of the condition, major problem lies in the decision of the treatment modality of these patients. Some authors propagate expectant management in asymptomatic patients and surgical removal of the cyst when the patient is symptomatic. In our patient, since the tracheal duplication cyst was identified intraoperatively at the time of TEF repair, surgical excision was done at the same time.

Incidentally noted intradiaphragmatic extralobar pulmonary sequestration during thoracoscopic repair of left eventration of diaphragm: Category: THORACIC
ReddyP Manoj KumarNarayanaK V SDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Niloufer Hospital, Hyderabad, Telangana, India
E-mail: MANOJJ.4892007@GMAIL.COM
Introduction: Intradiaphragmatic extralobar pulmonary sequestration (IDELPS) represents a unique subset of pulmonary sequestrations. We would like to present such a case of the left Congenital diaphragmatic eventeration in which IDELPS was noted during thoracoscopicrepair and was not picked up on an X-ray or contrast-enhanced computed tomography chest.
Presentation of the Case: A 3-month-old male child presented with a history of respiratory distress associated with fever for 2 weeks. On examination, abdomen was scaphoid and chest auscultation revealed decreased breath sounds in the left lower chest. Chest X-ray left dome of the diaphragmelevated. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography chest and abdomen-left eventration with superiorly located spleen stomach left kidney and splenic flexure of colon. Thoracoscopic plication of eventration planned. During plication incidentally noted an intrathoracic mass with long stalk adherent to the left diaphragm medially and free from the lung lobes on the left side.
Double ligation of the vascular supply and en mass excision was done. Biopsy revealed features of bronchoalveolar tissue, confirming the diagnosis.
Discussion: Embryologically, lungs bud from the developing foregut. Abnormal budding and separation of such abnormal buds from the normal tracheobronchial pathway, by definition, lead to a pulmonary sequestration. These anomalies have a separate systemic blood supply. Intraoperatively, finding the lesion with vascular supply from beneath the diaphragm and lesion being partially adhered to it, both could be dealt with precisely by a thoracoscopic approach.
Conclusion: Diaphragmatic eventration and extralobar pulmonary sequestration, both entities merit extensive radiological assessment to reveal the anatomic variations and guide the approach to surgery for appropriate management.
Role of anticholinergics: The futility of pharmaco-manipulation in bladder exstrophy epispadias complex: Category: UROLOGY
SinghApoorvBajpaiMinuAnandSachitDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: dr.singhapoorv@gmail.com
Objective: Bladder exstrophy-epispadias complex (BEEC) accounts for 1 in 50,000 live births. These patients are managed with the primary goal of providing adequate bladder closure while preserving the upper tracts. Although being used as first-line therapy in neurogenic bladders, anticholinergics are said to have suboptimal effects on the patients of BEEC in maintaining bladder pressures. This study aimed to assess the effects of anticholinergics in maintaining the bladder dynamics in patients with BEEC.
Methods: Of the 188 children managed at our institution between 1992 and 2019, 142 patients were included in the study. These patients had undergone either a staged repair (n = 65) or single-staged total reconstruction (n = 77). The patients were further divided into two groups– those who received anticholinergics (Oxybutynin) and those who did not receive the same. The patients were then evaluated with a urodynamic study (UDS) for measuring the measured cystometric capacity (MCC) and detrusor leak point pressure (LPP), and adequate statistical tests were applied to compare the mean MCC and mean LPP of both the groups.
Results: A total of 26 patients (11 in the staged group and 22 in the SSTR group) had received anticholinergic therapy. The mean MCC in the patients with anticholinergic use was 77.4 ± 33.8 ml versus 81.4 ± 30.0 ml in control patients (P = 0.877) with an LPP of 46.1 ± 15.5 cmH2O (anticholinergic) versus 51.2 ± 10.8 cmH2O (Control) (P = 0.769). Similarly, no difference was present in the SSTR group in terms of MCC (78.1 ± 33.4 ml vs. 83.2 ± 29.5 ml; P = 0.843) or LPP (45.3 ± 14.5 cmH2O vs. 52.1 ± 11.2; P = 0.689) in anticholinergic versus control groups.
Conclusions: Although having an established efficacy in patients with neurogenic bladder, the effectiveness of anticholinergics in maintaining bladder pressures and capacity in patients of BEEC is suboptimal and anticholinergic therapy shows no improvement in these patients when compared with controls.
Epispadias: A single-center experience on management and outcome: Category: UROLOGY
TotadriVarsha MAcharyaSamir KantPuriArchanaSugandhiNidhiJadhavAmitTeckchandaniNarinderSreedharanAnjanaSinghGauravVijiGThakurSonaliniChakrabortyAnwesaBeheraVikramKanodiaShrishtiVinodY V SShelkeVinayakumarDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Vardhman Mahavir Medical College and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: varsha2892@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to analyze the management of epispadias patients in a tertiary care center
Materials and Methods: Retrospective analysis of the management and outcome, of all cases of epispadias that were presented to our center, from 2018 to 2022
Results: Fourteen cases of epispadias were operated (Cantwell-Ransley procedure). 7 of 14 (50%) were isolated epispadias and remainder had Bladder exstrophy-epispadias complex (BEEC). 2 of 14 patients (14.2%) were 2 years or less. 4 of 14 patients (28.57%) had a phallus length of 2.5 cm or less. All 4 patients with short phallus had associated BEEC. All patients had ventral chordee. Of the 7 patients with isolated epispadias, 3 had penile type, 3-glanular type and 1-penopubic type. 3 of 14 patients (21.4%) developed urethrocutaneous fistula postsurgically. All the 3 patients who developed UC fistula had BEEC. 5 of 14 patients (isolated epispadias) (35.7%), had dressing opened on POD 7. Those with BEEC had dressings opened only between POD 9–10. Of the 7 patients who had BEEC, 4 had associated incontinence after 2nd stage surgery. All 7 of the isolated epispadias patients had preserved urinary continence. No chordee was noted postsurgically for any patient.
Conclusion: Epispadias repair requires precision to ensure cosmetic success and avoid recurrent surgeries. It is essential to ensure that chordee correction has been achieved postsurgically. A uniform operative technique should be used in all tertiary care centers to achieve a good outcome especially when pertaining to patients with BEEC due to their requirement of staged procedure.
The predictive factors for testicular presence in patients with unilateral nonpalpable undescended testis: Category: UROLOGY
PillaiAiswarya RAravindC SSethunathSBeenaS VSukumarPrathibaDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Government Medical College, Thiruvananthapuram, Kerala, India
E-mail: aiswarya.kairali@gmail.com
To study the predictive factors for testicular presence in patients with unilateral undescended testis
Methods: The data were collected retrospectively from hospital records, OT register, and discharge summaries of the patients.
Results: A total of 236 cases were included in the study. The mean age group involves 43.28 months. Among this 138 (58.5%) cases were left and 98(41.5%) cases were right-sided AIM: UDT. Out of 238 cases 142 (60.2%) patients had normal contralateral hemiscrotum and 94 (39.8%) has underdeveloped hemiscrotum.. 217 (91.9%) had empty scrotum while 19 (8.1%) had nubbin of tissue. Regarding the associated anomalies 211 (89.4) patients had isolated UDT with no associated anomalies however there are 12 cases of inguinal hernias and 1 case of Trisomy 21. 180 (76.3%) had no contralateral hypertrophy. 163 (69.1%) had normal intraabdominal testis while 44 (18.6%) had atrophic testis and 29 (12.3%) had vanishing testis.
Conclusion: Thus, the predictors of undescended testis as obtained in binary logistic regression are right side, undeveloped hemiscrotum, absence of C/L hypertrophy, and presence of hernia. Cox and snell R2 value of 0.710 indicates that 71% of undescended testis can be attributed to these four factors.
Occurrence of urethrocutaneous fistula following distal penile hypospadias repair using polyglactin 910 versus polydiaxone: Category: UROLOGY
KanodiaShrishtiVinodVenkata SaiTotadriVarsha MShelkeVinayakumarAcharyaSamir KPuriArchanaSugandhiNidhiJhadavAmit KDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Vardhaman Mahavir medical college and Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: shrishti_008@gmail.com
Introduction: Hypospadias is a common congenital anomaly with incidence of 3.2 in 1000 live male births or 1 in every 300 male children. Urethrocutaneous fistula (UCF) continues to be a common complication after hypospadias repair. Reported incidence of fistula formation varies from 0.48% to 44%. This remains a significant problem for pediatric surgeons despite many advances in surgical techniques.
Aim: The aim is to study the occurrence of UCF following distal penile hypospadias repair using polyglactin 910 versus polydioxanone.
Methodology: A randomized study carried out from 2021 to 2022 was carried out in the department of Paediatric Surgery. The study population included male child presenting with distal penile hypospadias. There were 2 study groups Group 1-PD-Polydiaxone and group 2 -PG-Polyglactin 910 group. Sample size was 30 in each group. PG-Polyglactin 910 group with a sample size of 30 in each group. All underwent Tubularized incised plate urethroplasty by a single surgeon.
Results: A total of 60 patients were included in the study and divided into two groups (2–12 years of age). 80% had well-developed glans, glanular tilt seen in 10% and 10% had underdeveloped glans. The urethral plate was narrow in 18%. 10 of total patients had UCF out of which 20% were from the PD group and 80% were from PG group(P < 0.04).
Conclusion: There was a significant difference seen between the incidences of UCF after hypospadias repairs done in the two groups PD and PG for the urethral closure in our study. long-term follow up is needed to look for occurrence of meatal stenosis in these patients.
Keywords: Hypospadias, polydiaxone, polyglactin-910, urethrocutaneous fistula
Lower urinary tract outcomes following augmentation gastrocystoplasty for bladder exstrophy: Category: UROLOGY
AnandSuramyaBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery,All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: suramya.anand@gmail.com
Aim: The study aimed to analyze the lower urinary tract outcomes following augmentation gastrocystoplasty in pediatric patients with bladder exstrophy.
Methods: In this retrospective study, 142 children < 12 years old who had undergone either staged (n = 65) or complete primary repair of exstrophy (n = 77) were included. Of these, 48 patients were started on anticholinergics and 33 underwent augmentation with a gastric patch. Demographic data and data pertaining to lower urinary tract outcomes based on urodynamic study (UDS) done at different intervals postrepair, post anticholinergic medication, and postaugmentation are analyzed and presented.
Results: Postrepair UDS in all patients showed small bladder capacity and high leak point pressures that did not improve on follow-up or with anticholinergic medication. Postaugmentation gastrocystoplasty there was a significant increase in maximum cystometric capacity (82.5 ± 16.3 mL to 341.2 ± 44.5 mL), and a decrease in leak point pressure (36.81 ± 10.5 cmH2O to 27.96 ± 14.7 cmH2O). Complications included dysuria (10.3%), hematuria (7%), and dribbling and stress incontinence (7%)
Conclusion: Augmentation gastrocystoplasty is a safe and viable option in the creation of a large and compliant urinary reservoir in children with exstrophy bladder.
Is the threshold for eligibility of safe bladder capacity for bladder neck reconstruction an unrealistic target in bladder exstrophy?: Category: UROLOGY
JadhavShishirBajpaiM.AnandSachitDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, New Delhi, India
E-mail: jadhavshishir03@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to compare the functional outcomes of children with Bladder capacity (BC) of ≥ 100 ml vs those with BC of < 100 ml in operated bladder exstrophypatients.
Materials and Methods: Children with BE operated between 1992 and 2019 were included after 5 years of follow-up. They were categorized into those with BC > 100 ml (group A) and BC < 100 ml (group B). Upper and lower urinary tract functions were evaluated.
Results: Out of 142 cases of BE operated, 42 had BC of ≥ 100 ml. Of these, 24 had undergone SSTR, 18 were staged repair. Upper tract damage in terms of the of cases with GFR < 79 ml/min/BSA, the proportion of renal units with Split renal function < 35%, and the proportion of renal units with cortical scarring did not differ among the two groups. The urodynamic study showed DLPP > 40 cm of H2O in the >50% of the cases and did not differ among the two patient groups. The proportion of renal units with cortical scarring or the proportion of cases with GFR <79 ml/min/BSA was significantly higher in Single stage reconstruction versus staged group.
Conclusions: The upper and lower tract outcomes in children with a BC of 100 ml or more are not significantly different from those with BC of < 100 ml. Irrespective of the type of procedure, these low capacity-high pressure systems lead to upper tract damage.
Upper tract outcomes following bladder exstrophy-epispadias complex repair: Category: UROLOGY
JhaVimalenduBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: docvjha@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to assess the upper tract outcomes following Bladder Exstrophy-Epispadias complex (BEEC) repair.
Materials and Methods: Retrospective study conducted on patients undergoing repair of BEEC with different procedures under a single surgeon. The presence of split renal function (<35%), renal cortical scarring on DMSA, high-grade vesicoureteric reflux (VUR > Grade II), and glomerular filtration rate (GFR) (>80 ml/min) were documented as parameters to assess the upper tract damage.
Results: 142 patients of BEEC underwent Repair with a mean follow-up of 5 years. The staged repair was performed in 65/142 patients (45.7%) while 77/142 patients (54.3%) underwent single-stage total reconstruction (SSTR). The mean duration of follow-up was 5 years for the staged group and 2.5 years for the SSTR group. Evidence of upper tract damage based on the various parameters observed was seen in both the groups– Split Renal Function < 35% in 30% of patients undergoing Staged repair vs 10.7% of SSTR, renal cortical scarring in 30.8% of Staged repair versus 16.7% of SSTR patients, High-grade VUR in 42% of Staged repair and 10.3% of SSTR patients while GFR < 80 ml/hr was observed in 65.9% of patients undergoing Staged repair versus 33.3% of those undergoing SSTR. The decline in GFR started much early in SSTR group, however, the final GFR values showed no significant difference between the two groups.
Conclusions: Irrespective of the type of procedure, staged or SSTR, there is a definite damage to the upper tracts in these patients as the follow-up period progresses.

InayathLamiaManeS BDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Grant government medical college and Sir JJ group of Hospitals
E-mail: lamiinayath@gmail.com
Aim: This study aims to compare the outcomes of single- and two-stage repairs in the treatment of proximal penile hypospadias
Materials and Methods: This is a retrospective study comparing the outcomes of single- and two-stage repairs in proximal penile hypospadias. In the period of 2017–2022, 55 patients with proximal penile hypospadias have been operated in our institution.
Results: The mean age of presentation was 5.5 years. As per our study, 41 patients underwent single stage and 14 patients underwent two-stage repair. Analysis was done based on the type of single-stage and two-stage repairs and the complications associated with each type of repair.
Conclusion: There was no significant difference in the complication rate following single (34%) or two-stage (35%) repairs. There was no recurrence of chordee in any of the patients included in the study. The main advantage with single-stage repair was a reduced number of surgeries, whereas with two-stage surgeries, it is very effective in patients planned for redo urethroplasties. Surgeon preference also plays a major role in deciding the type of surgery planned for proximal penile hypospadias.
Mini-PCNL and RIRS in toddlers – Our experience: Category: UROLOGY
BabuRameshPrakashJ SanjayThangarasuMathisekaranVenkatsubramaniamDArunkumarBDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Asian Institute of Nephrology and Urology, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: drrameshbabu1@gmail.com
Aims: The aim is to report our experience of managing complex urolithiasis in toddlers using minimally invasive techniques and share the video of successful mini Percutaneous Nephrolithotomy (mPCNL) and Retrograde Intra-Renal Surgery (RIRS)
Methods: Case 1: A 2-year-old boy presented with a history of hematuria and Urinary Tract Infection (UTI). Computerized tomography (CT) scan confirmed a large left-sided staghorn calculus with two further fragments in calyces. Intravenous venous urogram (IVU) revealed undilated system packed with stones. Using mini PCNL and holmium laser, the stone was fragmented completely. A double J stent was kept for 6 weeks
Case 2: A 3-year-old girl presented with a history of recurrent UTI. Ultrasound and CT scan revealed a bladder stone of 8 mm size; in addition, there was a 1.2 cm partial staghorn calculus on the right side with an extension into the right lower calyx. There was an additional small fragment in the right lower calyx and 4 mm lower calyceal stone. Using cystoscopy and laser, bladder calculus was fragmented. RIRS was done to clear the renal and calyceal fragments. A double J stent was kept for 6 weeks
Results: Both patients were discharged within 36 h and had an uneventful recovery. At the time of stent removal, there was complete clearance of calculi. Stone analysis, metabolic evaluation results, and a review of our experience will be presented at the conference.
Conclusion: RIRS and mPCNL are the ideal minimally invasive modalities in toddlers with complex urolithiasis. Selection of appropriate treatment is crucial to get full clearance and a speedy recovery.

POD following pyeloplasty in unilateral UPJO: Category: UROLOGY
AshitoshPokharkarGuptaAmitChadhaRajivChoudhuryS RSarinYogesh KKhannaVikramDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Lady Hardinge Medical College and Associated Kalawati Saran Children's Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: ashitoshpd106@gmail.com
Aims: The aim is to study the incidence and characteristics of postobstructive diuresis (POD) following pyeloplasty for unilateral pelvic-ureteric junction obstruction (PUJO) with a normal contralateral kidney. To correlate the diuresis with the renal function and severity of hydronephrosis.
Methods: This prospective observational cross-sectional study included children with unilateral PUJO and normal contralateral kidney with no other urological abnormalities. Preoperatively, ultrasonography findings and renal scintigraphy scans were noted. All patients underwent standard Anderson–Hynes pyeloplasty with a transanastomotic stent, a nephrostomy tube, and a perinephric drain. Nephrostomy output represented the urine from the affected kidney while the periurethral catheter represented urine from the contralateral normal kidney. On postoperative days 1, 3, and 5, urine volume, specific gravity, urinary electrolytes, and creatinine were documented separately for both kidneys.
Results: Thirty patients were included in the study with an M:F ratio of 3.2:1 and the median age was 11 months (IQR = 16.5 m). The mean urine output (nearly two times), and mean fractional excretion of electrolytes were significantly higher in the affected kidney than in the normal kidney with significantly lower specific gravity (all P < 0.001). Nineteen (63.33%) patients had POD which subsided within 48 h in 14 (73.7%) patients and by day 5, POD resolved in all patients. The mean APD was significantly higher in patients with POD than in patients without POD (P < 0.001). The mean parenchymal thickness was significantly lower in the POD group than in the group without POD (P = 0.008). There was no statistically significant difference in differential renal function between both groups (P = 0.749).
Conclusion: Diuresis after the relief of unilateral obstruction seems invariable. Patients with larger APD and thinner renal parenchyma are at higher risk of significant POD. Further studies are required to assess the severity and clinical implications of POD in such patients.
Role of check cystoscopy after initial posterior urethral valves fulguration: Category: UROLOGY
DRashmiMashalSahilGautamVasuKhannaChetnaDebnathPinaki RanjanKundalVijay KumarDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, ABVIMS and Dr Ram Manohar Lohia hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: rashmi.dotihalkar@gmail.com
Aims: Posterior urethral valves (PUV) is the most common form of congenital urethral obstruction (with an incidence of 1/3000–1/8000 male births) and the most common congenital abnormality giving rise to bilateral renal obstruction. Endoscopic fulguration of PUV using Bugbee/hook electrode, Nd YAG laser is the gold standard therapy. In this study, we have standardized by performing a re-look/check cystoscopy after 3 months of initial PUV fulguration.
Methods: In this retrospective study, 90 cases that underwent PUV fulguration were included. The need for check cystoscopy was based on the clinical features, ultrasonography, and confirmed by MCU. The check cystoscopy was performed after 3 months of initial PUV fulguration, to look for residual valves. Risk factors were evaluated.
Results: The mean age at presentation was 3 years. The most common symptoms were poor stream, persistent straining, persistent dribbling, breakthrough UTI, and urinary retention. Repeat cystoscopy was performed in 36 patients after 3 months of valve fulguration, in which residual valves were seen in 21 patients. Post residual valve fulguration urinary symptoms improved, the incidence of breakthrough UTI decreased and there was improvement in radiological parameters as well.
Conclusion: Preoperative renal scars, VUR, and HDUN are high-risk factors for residual valves. A large number of patients had residual valve on repeat cystoscopy. Post residual fulguration, patients improved clinically and radiologically. Hence, we suggest check cystoscopy in high-risk patients after initial fulguration to look for residual valves.
Double trouble in ureter: Challenges in diagnosis and management: Category: UROLOGY
BMamathaAlladiAnandDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Bangalore Mdical College and Research Institute, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India
E-mail: mamathanaveen3@gmail.com
Aim: This study aims to assess the difficulties in diagnosis and management of double obstruction in ipsilateral ureter and review literature on their management protocols.
Materials and Methods: A retrospective study from April 2013 to April 2022 all children 0–12 years treated for pelviureteric junction obstruction and vesicoureteric junction (VUJ) obstruction in ipsilateral ureter were included. Data were collected from electronic database, Op, Ip, and OT registers. We excluded patient with PUV, neurogenic bladder, and urethral strictures.
Results: There were a total of 7 patients with double obstruction in ipsilateral ureter with a male-to-female ratio of 6:1. 3 of the 7 were detected while doing pyeloplasty while the remaining 4 were detected while doing standard imaging protocol in primary obstructive megaureter. First 3 patients were out of 8 patients undergoing pyeloplasty in whom stent could not be passed across VUJ. 5 of them had no further issues while the remaining 3 were diagnosed to have associated VUJO and underwent some intervention (2 reimplant and 1 VUJ dilatation). Of 23 units of primary obstructive megaureter, 4 patients during the evaluation were found to have associated PUJO. They needed intervention at both levels. MRU, diuretic renogram, and RGP done in all 4 patients. 2 underwent lap-assisted ureterostomy and lap-assisted pyeloplasty during the same time, 1 underwent lap-assisted ureterostomy followed by pyeloplasty after 8 weeks, both awaiting reimplant. 1 underwent pyeloplasty and VUJ stenting. Of the 7 patients, 4 have shown improvement in function and 3 awaiting diuretic scan post-surgery.
Conclusion: Children with a double obstruction present with challenges in diagnosis and management. In patients with PUJO, associated VUJO is usually incidentally picked up during or after pyeloplasty. In patients with POM-associated PUJO should be evaluated and MRU/Diuretic renogram, RGP, and laparoscopy give valuable information regarding the same. The management depends on the presentation and can be successfully managed with staged surgeries
Assessing the validity of TWIST score for diagnosing testicular torsion in children: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis: Category: UROLOGY
AgarwalPriyanjaliChoudhuryPrativaGoelPrabudhJainVisheshYadavDevendra KumarDhuaAnjan KumarAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, New Delhi, India
E-mail: priyanjali.agarwal79@gmail.com
Aim: Assessing the validity of TWIST score for diagnosing testicular torsion in children-a Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.
Methods: We systematically searched the electronic databases such as PubMed, Scopus, and Google scholar including studies measuring testicular torsion, testis, and TWIST scores. Based on our inclusion and exclusion criteria, 17 articles were included for the review following the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses guidelines.
Results: Data were extracted for 16 included studies in Microsoft Excel spreadsheets. Nine studies were included for the meta-analysis in Review Manager 5.4.1 software. Sensitivity was perfect (100%) in three studies, 70%–90% in four studies, 50% in one study, and 20% in one study of the nine studies. Specificity was perfect (100%) in six studies, 90% in two studies, and 50% in one study of the total nine studies. The calculated cumulative sensitivity and specificity were 83.6% and 91.2% of the included nine studies. Summary ROC (SROC) was calculated and the graph shows a cumulative high sensitivity and high specificity for the majority of the studies indicating the TWIST score as an excellent tool in the diagnosis of testicular torsion.
Conclusion: The study showed that the TWIST score is a validated and reliable score that can be used in the diagnosis of testicular torsion. It is concluded that based on the sensitivity and specificity analysis and SROC analysis, the TWIST score is a useful tool in the diagnosis of testicular torsion.
The mitrofanoff procedure: Our experience with a 7-year follow-up: Category: UROLOGY
AchugatlaSushmaSharmaRahul DeoSinghSurendraKarmakarSantoshLilavati Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: sushmaachugatla10@gmail.com
Aim: Retrospective analysis of our experience of the Mitrofanoff procedure with a 7-year follow up
Materials and Methods: 35 patients, with mean age of 8 years (6 years to 21 years) in whom urinary continence repair was performed between december 2006 and july 2017 were included. The parameters analyzed were patient demography, indications, site of Mitrofanoff, the type of conduit and the technique used, success rate, and complications. The mean follow-up was 7 years.
Results: Out of 35 children, 21 males and 14 females. Spina bifida patients (27), sacral agenesis (4), postoperative cases of anorectal malformations (2) and posterior urethral valve(PUV) (1), operated case of bladder exstrophy (1). Right-sided (35), left -sided (1) were performed. Initial management was done conservatively by clean intermittent catheterization before the Mitrofanoff procedure. The most common method used was using the distal appendix as a catheterizable conduit (appendicovesicostomy) with anti-reflux mechanism. In 2 patients, bladder tube was made. In one case of PUV, left-sided mitrofanoff with bladder tube was made. All the procedures were successful with no immediate postoperative complications. On follow-up, the Mitrofanoff channel is working well in 34/35 patients. Among the complications, 1 patient had difficulty in catheterizing with narrowing of the channel seen at the appendicovesicostomy junction, which was managed by a new left-sided Mitrofanoff channel. Stomal site stitch abscess seen in three patients, which was managed by drainage of the abscess and minor refashioning of the stoma. There was reflux of urine was seen in one of the patients.
Conclusion: Mitrofanoff (right side or left side) usually using appendix as a catheterizable conduit with an anti-reflux mechanism is an effective procedure in the management of urinary incontinence. When appendix is not available, a bladder tube procedure can be performed.
Mini-percutaneous nephrolithotomy – Effective method to manage obstructive uropathy in children: Category: UROLOGY
SinghSurendraSharmaRahul DeoAchugatlaSushmaRedkarRajeevLilavati Hospital and Research Centre, Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: drsurendrasinghkgmu@gmail.com
Introduction: Urolithiasis presents 1 in 1754 children. Its not an uncommon problem and is on rise due to changing lifestyle. Urolithiasis if not managed properly, can lead to severe problems such as recurrent infections, scarring of kidneys, and renal failure. We are reporting a case of female child having renal stone managed by relatively new and advanced technique Mini Percutaneous Nephrolithotomy (PCNL) (mini perc).
Case Report: A Four-year-old female admitted with complaints of abdominal pain and hematuria for 2 days. On examination, the patient was afebrile and abdominal examination was normal. Blood investigations were normal. The urine routine showed blood +, urine culture showed no growth. Computed tomography abdomen reported a single calculus measuring 8 mm× 5.3 mm (1600 HU) seen in the left renal pelvis. Mini-PCNL (holmium laser lithotripsy using 550 nm fiber, 0.8 Joule energy and 20 Hz frequency) was done along with DJ stenting with the help of mini nephroscope. The stone analysis was reported as calcium oxalate stone. Postprocedure, ultrasound KUB reported no renal calculi. D-J stent was removed after 2 weeks. The patient was advised dietary modification-low oxalate diet on discharge. On follow-up, the patient is asymptomatic.
Conclusion: The Mini-PCNL although challenging technically but is a safe and effective way to manage obstructive urolithiasis.
