Neurogenic bladder and bowel: An audit of 50 cases in a tertiary center: Category: UROLOGY
HeeraTRamachandranPriyaDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Kanchi Kamakoti CHILDS trust hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: heeratharanendran@gmail.com
Introduction: Spinal anomalies such as Spina bifida-meningomyelocele, myelodysplasia, and Tethered cord syndrome are associated with neurogenic abnormalities of the bladder and bowel. These children need a complete evaluation to detect anatomical and functional abnormalities of the urinary tract. The treatment goals are aimed at the preservation of the kidneys and to achieve bladder and bowel continence. A minority of children will not respond to conservative therapy and will ultimately require surgical intervention.
Aim: This study aims to study the bladder and bowel profile in children who underwent surgery for primary spinal anomalies.
Methods: A retrospective analysis was done on children with neurogenic bladder and bowel after spinal surgery who reported to our surgical outpatient department. Patient data including demographic details, clinical profile, continence profile, renal function (urea and Creatinine) ultrasonogram, DMSA scan, voiding cystourethrogram (VCUG), and urodynamic study were recorded. Treatment given was noted.
Results: A total of 50 patients were reviewed. Ultrasonography showed that all patients had bladder wall thickening and 70% had bilateral hydronephrosis. AVCUG showed that 20% had VUR and DMSA scan revealed bilateral renal scarring in 10%. The urodynamic study showed that the majority of the patients had small capacity, high-pressure bladders. Most of them were on CIC and anticholinergics. Complete dryness was achieved only in 4% with conservative management. 4 children developed early renal failure. Despite medical management, 3 children underwent surgical treatment (augmentation cystoplasty-1, vesicostomy-1, and ureterostomy-1).
Conclusion: Despite nonsurgical treatment, dryness is not achieved in a majority of these patients. Although we were able to preserve the kidney function with medical management, the quality of life can be enhanced only with surgical procedures such as bladder augmentation, Mitrofanoff and bladder neck reconstruction surgery, and antegrade continent enemas procedures.
Role of check cystoscopy in posterior urethral valves: Category: UROLOGY
HeeraTMadhuRKSenthil GaneshLakshmiSDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Kanchi Kamakoti CHILDS Trust Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: heeratharanendran@gmail.com
Background: The management of posterior urethral valves (PUV) bladder remains a real challenge in the long-term to protect the kidneys. The relative merits of assessment of residual valves by MCU or check cystoscopy are constantly debated. In this study, we aimed to highlight our experience with check cystoscopy in PUV patients.
Methods: We did a retrospective analysis of PUV cases managed by 3 independent consultants. All patients underwent check cystoscopy between 6 and 12 weeks to assess for residual valves irrespective of their clinical improvement.
Results: Our study included a total of 30 cases. 28/30 (93%) were noted to have residual valve leaflets on re-look cystoscopy which were re-fulgurated. No patient had stricture at PUV site.
Conclusion: Residual valves are identified in a very high proportion of children undergoing check cystoscopy. Hence, it should be an integral part of PUV management protocol.
Utility of tissue sealant as an adjunct during urethroplasty in children with hypospadias: A systematic review and meta-analysis: Category: UROLOGY
KrishnanNellaiSinghApoorvAnandSachitGoelPrabudhYadavDevendra KumarJainVisheshDhuaAnjan KumarBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Aim: Tissue sealant application can provide additional support to the suture line and can prevent operative failure. This systematic review and meta-analysis aim to compare the outcomes of hypospadias surgery, in terms of its complications, in children with and without tissue sealant application.
Methods: The authors systematically searched the databases PubMed, EMBASE, Web of Science, and Scopus. The proportion of children developing overall complications after sealant use was the primary outcome of this study. Statistical analysis was performed using a random-effects model, and pooled risk ratio (RR) and heterogeneity (I2) were calculated.
Results: Four comparative studies were included in the meta-analysis. Different types of tissue sealants such as fibrin glue, cyanoacrylate glue, BioGlue, and cryocalcium glue were used in these studies. As compared to without sealant application, hypospadias repair performed with sealant application showed no significant difference in the incidence of overall postoperative complications (RR 0.63; 95% confidenc interval [CI] = 0.34–1.14, P = 0.13). However, a significant reduction in some specific complications including urethrocutaneous fistula (RR 0.37; 95% CI 0.19–0.72, P = 0.003), wound-related complications (RR 0.57; 95% CI 0.38–0.86, P = 0.008) and complications involving the neourethra (RR 0.15; 95% CI 0.04–0.54, P = 0.004) was observed with sealant usage.
Conclusions: The present meta-analysis revealed no additional benefit of the repair of hypospadias performed with tissue sealants than without sealants in terms of reducing the overall postoperative complications. However, a significant reduction in the occurrence of some specific complications, including UCF, wound-related complications, and complications involving the neourethra was observed with sealant application.
Comparative study on tunica vaginalis flap used as an additional layer in urethroplasty with single layer cover: Category: UROLOGY
MohanPreethi KJadavVinayBabuNarendraRameshSDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Indira Gandhi Institute of Child Health, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India
E-mail: preethikmohan@gmail.com
Aim: Hypospadias, resulting from the disruption of the normal urethral formation, is one of the most common congenital malformations in male infants, with an overall predicted prevalence of 20.9 cases per 10,000 male live births around the world. Additional urethral coverage is now routinely used by most pediatric urologists to reduce the incidence of postoperative complications
Methods: We have compared 26 patients who have undergone tunica vaginalis flap cover as an additional layer to 26 patients who have undergone conventional urethral cover over infant feeling tube in hypospadias and epispadias repair to create neourethra operated from June 2021 to June 2022. Both groups were compared for cosmetics outcomes and other postoperative complications such as fistula and urine stream.
Results: Twenty-six patients in study group (T) who underwent tunica vaginalis flap as an additional cover for urethroplasty. The control group consisted of 26 patients. The urethroplasty in both groups was done for mainly proximal and mid-penile hypospadias. Others with distal hypospadias, epispadias, and rectouretheral fistula included. Staged repair and primary tip repair also included. The mean age group (3 years) was comparable in both groups and followed up for cosmetic outcomes; fistula formation and urine stream. Two patients in study group developed skin wound gape with underlying urethral cover intact. Five patients in the control group developed fistula. There was no testicular complications noted in the study group
Conclusion: Owing to its good vascular supply and reproducible simple technique of raising tunica vaginalis flap gives an advantage in using it as an additional layer in urethroplasty. However, due to the risk of testicular injury and injury to vas structures meticulous dissection is needed. long-term follow-up is needed.
Diversions in PUV: Challenges in resource-limited settings: Category: UROLOGY
BanerjeeRupaVizPoonam GuhaBasuKalyaniDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Nil Ranta Sircar Medical College and Hospital
E-mail: dctrrupa@gmail.com
Aim: We report a case of PUV with hugely dilated pelvis and ureters not seen and complications with high diversions.
Case Report: Two-day-old male baby born at 34 weeks with birth weight of 2.5 kg presented with huge abdominal distension, passing very little urine, not passed stool, and sepsis. No significant antenatal history. On per abdomen examination bilateral kidneys and bladder were palpable. The baby was catheterized and further investigated. ultrasonography KUB showed hugely distended renal pelvis with a thin cortex but ureters were not visualized. MCUG– hugely dilated posterior urethra with very bad trabeculated bladder with diverticula. No VUR. The baby was catheterized and observed for a week. No significant post obstructive diuresis. No significant change in serum creatinine level. Repeat ultrasonography KUB– Not much change seen. Cystoscopy was tried but failed. He underwent right loop ureterostomy. The intraoperative ureter was not dilated and proximally there was? Secondary PUJO and we also did tube nephrostomy. He still had urosepsis and later we did left loop ureterostomy where we brought out PUJ as stoma. Later, he was discharged in stable condition. At 3 months’ age he again had UTI and right nephrostogram was done after treating infection. It confirmed the diagnosis of right PUJO and right loop ureterostomy was converted to Sobers after straightening the ureter. At 5 months of age we did cystoscopy and valve fulgration. At present, he is infection free for the last 4 months. He is passing urine per urethra and left ureterostomy is functioning whereas right sobers ureterostomy is partially stenosed. Further plan is to get a DTPA scan and plan for pyeloplasty.
Conclusions: In cases of PUV, where the pelvis is disproportionately dilated than the ureter we should think about the associated PUJO. In these cases, pyelostomy is a better option than ureterostomy.
Gastric neobladder: Our experience so far: Category: UROLOGY
SehgalMehakBajpaiMinuGoelPrabudhDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: mehak.sehgal91@gmail.com
Aim: One of the novel therapeutic options in the management of the bladder in patients with exstrophy-epispadias complex, to improve the quality of life, is a creation of a gastric neo-bladder. In this study, we aim to analyze the outcomes of this procedure in pediatric patients with bladder exstrophy-epispadias complex.
Methods: Patients of exstrophy-epispadias complex, post-primary single stage or staged repair, who underwent a creation of gastric neo-bladder, were evaluated over 4.5 years. The surgical technique will be discussed in detail.
Results: A total of 7 patients were included in the study. The male-to-female ratio was 5:2. The mean age at undergoing the procedure was 10.7 years (7.5-13 years). Complications were noted in 4 patients in the postoperative period. The mean bladder volume on dye study done on postoperative day 21 was 95 ml. The final bladder volume achieved was significantly higher at 9 months than the initial bladder volume achieved during the postoperative period (mean 550 ml vs. 95 ml). Clean intermittent catheterization (CIC) was possible in all patients except 1, who had difficulty in catheterization. The frequency of CIC was 4–6 h at follow-up, which was season and intake dependent. All patients were reported to be dry and continent at follow-up.
Conclusion: The initial results by the creation of a gastric neo-bladder have led to a significant improvement in continence and bladder volumes, with promising outcomes. It is a viable option in the management of exstrophy-epispadias patients, with the creation of a large and compliant urinary reservoir.
Nomogram of renal dimensions in pediatric patients in the indian cohort: Category: UROLOGY
MukherjeeSampreetiMukherjeeSampreetiGoelPrabudhVermaVivekJainVisheshDhuaAnjanYadavDevender KumarAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: sampreetimukherjee@gmail.com
Background: Most of the literature pertaining to the “normal” dimensions of kidney come from Western literature and studies on adult population. Residing in a developing country, we rely heavily on cheaper investigations like ultrasound, instead of more costly alternatives like computed tomography/magnetic resonance imaging. Most of the critical decisions in patient management such as renal failure determination, frequency of follow-up, need for transplant, and patient counseling depends heavily on the renal parameters measured via ultrasonography. Thus, there is a dire need to have clear-cut data to help categorize patients into diseased or nondiseased groups.
Aim: To identify the age-based renal parameters among children of 0–19 years’ age group and create a standard nomogram for the pediatric population in the Indian subcontinent.
Methodology: A targeted sample size of 100 children per group (each group spreading across 1 year) was calculated. Various renal dimensions were recorded using ultrasonography for each child, including Renal length, width, thickness, anatomical location, orientation, and angle of rotation. For data analysis, generalized additive models for Location, Scale, and Shape were used. Non-parametric smoothing cubic spline functions of age with five effective degrees of freedom with Box-Cox t distribution were adopted for model fitting. Based on the best-fitted model, the centiles curves of 3, 5, 10, 25, 50, 75, 90, 95, and 97 over age were calculated.
Results: The nomogram for the various renal dimensions across the pediatric age range is herewith generated and presented.
Nomogram of serum testosterone and dihydrotestosterone in pediatric patients in the indian cohort: Category: UROLOGY
MukherjeeSampreetiMukherjeeSampreetiGoelPrabudhVermaVivekJainVisheshDhuaAnjanYadavDevender KumarAnandSachitBajpaiMinuDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institue of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: sampreetimukherjee@gmail.com
Background: Testosterone and dihydrotestosterone (DHT) formulations are routinely used in pediatric surgery to increase penile girth and length preoperatively in patients with hypospadias. However, the data available for normal values of serum testosterone is limited to adult population or in populations with a wide age range. Thus, there is a dire need of a study on these hormone levels that will help distinguish between what is normal and abnormal.
Aim: To identify the age-based testosterone and DHT levels among children of 1–14 years age group and create a standard nomogram for the pediatric population in the Indian subcontinent.
Methodology: A targeted sample size of 100 children per group (each group spreading across 1 year) was calculated. The serum testosterone levels and serum dihydro testosterone levels were recorded for each child. Generalized additive models for Location, Scale, and Shape were used. Non-parametric smoothing cubic spline functions of age with five effective degrees of freedom with Box-Cox t distribution for testosterone and DHT were adopted for model fitting. Based on the best-fitted model, the centiles curves of 3, 5, 10, 25, 50, 75, 90, 95, and 97 over age were calculated for testosterone and DHT levels.
Results: The nomogram for testosterone and dihydrotestosterone across the pediatric age range is herewith generated and presented.
Posterior urethral polyps – A case series: Category: UROLOGY
MalviyaSonalParelkarSandeshDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Seth G S and KEM hospital Mumbai, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: sonal.malviyaigmc@gmail.com
Introduction: Urethral polyps are a rare finding in young children. They are benign fibroepithelial lesions with no tendency to recur. They are usually diagnosed during the first decade of life. The presentation can be with obstruction, voiding dysfunction, and hematuria. Cysto-urethroscopy is the investigation of choice for final diagnosis. Post confirmation of diagnosis, they are treated by surgical ablation, fulguration, or laser therapy. We report three cases of urethral polyps. Two of our patients were infants. One of our patients presented with excessive crying during micturition and other presented with lower urinary tract symptoms. The third patient was diagnosed incidentally while undergoing diagnostic cystoscopy for antenatally diagnosed bilateral hydroureteronephrosis. One patient was found to have multiple polyps. All three patients underwent endoscopic management.
Conclusion: Though uncommon, urethral polyps should be included in the differential diagnosis of any boy presenting with symptoms of lower urinary tract such as urinary retention or hematuria. Cysto-uretheroscopic management is the treatment of choice for these polyps.
A case of posttraumatic urethral distraction defect – Multiple redo surgeries: Category: UROLOGY
SinghGarvitaSinghGauravVarmaMuniAggarwalSatish KumarDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: garvitasingh429@gmail.com
Introduction: We present a case of post-traumatic urethral distraction defect with previously failed urethroplasty (twice), with rectourethral fistula managed with a PAPA + transanorectal approach for resection of scarred urethra and Monte's tube urethral replacement.
Case Report: A 9-year-old boy who sustained multiple injuries in a road traffic accident, 4 years back, was managed initially with orthopedic procedures for fracture left nek of femur and pubic rami. He sustained urethral injury secondary to pubic rami fracture for which he underwent perineal end-to-end anastomotic urethroplasty followed by cystopanendoscopy and optical internal urethrotomy. He again underwent OIU for recurrent urethral stricture followed by a transpubic elaborated perineal uretroplasty at 7 years of age. Post-surgery he developed rectourethral fistula and a transverse colostomy was performed. Following multiple failed redo surgeries he presented to us and an antegrade and retrograde cystoscopy was performed along with a gapogram. A gap of 7–8 cm was there with a distal normal urethra of only 4 cm length. He underwent resection of scarred urethra (proximal penile/bulb/membranous), repair of rectourethral fistula with anterior rectoplasty, and urethral replacement with Monte's tube from proximal sigmoid/descending colon. Based on the marginal arcade supplied by the superior rectal, a 10 cm long pedicle was harvested and a Monte tube of 8 cm length made with ~20 Fr caliber. The pedicle was brought to perineum, peile urethra was mobilised and circumferential anastomosis was performed at both ends. Colostomy was closed and a diverting ileostomy was made which was closed at a later date. The child recovered well.
Conclusion: Previously multiple failed surgeries pose many technical difficulties and are bound to take more operative time. In a long-gap urethral stricture, a urethral replacement using bowel (Monte segment) is a well-established option.
Dual obstruction of ureter: A diagnostic challenge: Category: UROLOGY
KalagotlaChandrikaAggarwalSatish KumarVarmaMuniAggarwalGarvita SinghVermaVarshaDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: drkchandrika2k9@gmail.com
Introduction: Isolated obstruction of the pelviureteric junction and the vesicoureteric junction are the two most common causes of hydronephrosis in a pediatric population. They do not pose diagnostic difficulties when present alone but when together can be difficult to diagnose. Here, we discuss the problems we faced when we encountered these two anomalies in the same ureter and the way in which we managed them.
Aim: This stusdy aims to assess the difficulties in the diagnosis of pediatric patients who present with both pelviureteric junction obstruction (PUJO) and vesicoureteric junction obstruction (VUJO) in the ipsilateral ureter and their management protocol.
Materials and Methods: This is a retrospective study from January 2017 to December 2021. Out of 200 children who were diagnosed to have hydronephrosis due to PUJO in our institute, 5 patients (in the age range of 2 months of age to 17 years) had both UPJO and VUJO in the ipsilateral ureter. The problems we faced in diagnosing the two conditions are mentioned with a literature review.
Results: Operative intervention was used in all the patients; none of the patients had an accurate diagnosis before surgery. All patients were suspected of having double obstruction during pyeloplasty when the appropriate size double J stent could not be negotiated through the vesicoureteric junction into the bladder. Postoperative nephrostogram and MR urogram confirmed the diagnosis in all patients.
Conclusion: Children with double obstruction of the ipsilateral ureter present a diagnostic dilemma. Because of the rarity of this condition, it can be difficult to diagnose. Early diagnosis can be made if this condition is kept in mind while treating any hydronephrosis due to UPJO or UVJO.
A qualitative study of smooth muscle cell characteristics and myofibroblasts in processus vaginalis of pediatric inguinal hernia, hydrocele, and undescended testis: Category: UROLOGY
KrishnanNellaiDhuaAnjanGoelPrabudhAgarwalaSandeepKakkarAanchalNagTapas ChandraDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India
E-mail: nellai93@gmail.com
Aim: To compare the characteristics of smooth muscle cell and myofibroblasts in the processus vaginalis of pediatric inguinal hernia, hydrocele and undescended testis (UDT).
Methods: A cross-sectional study was performed in children up to 5 years of age with inguinal hernia, hydrocele, and undescended testis. Herniotomy was performed and the processus vaginalis was subjected to histopathological examination, immunohistochemistry (IHC) staining for vimentin, desmin and smooth muscle actin (SMA) and electron microscopy to look for myofibroblasts. Peritoneal samples from age-matched patients served as controls.
Results: Thirty-seven children have been included, of which 21 (2 females) were inguinal hernia, 9 hydrocele and 7 UDT. Smooth muscle bundles were present in 17/21 (81%) of inguinal hernia, 7/9 (78%) of hydrocele and absent in all cases of UDT. On IHC, desmin was positive in 18/21 (86%) of inguinal hernia, 8/9 (89%) of hydrocele, and 2/7 (28%) of UDTs. SMA was positive in 15/21 (71%) of inguinal hernia, 7/9 (78%) of hydrocele, and absent in undescended testis. Vimentin was positive in 1/21 (4.7%) of inguinal hernia, 2/9(22%) of hydrocele, and 3/7 (42%) of UDT. Electron microscopy showed the presence of myofibroblasts in all sacs of inguinal hernia, 3/9 (33%) of hydrocele, and absent in UDT.
Conclusion: The processus vaginalis of inguinal hernia shows smooth muscle bundles of mature IHC phenotype and presence of myofibroblasts as compared to hydrocele and UDT, thereby leading to difference in clinical presentation.
Ureteric stenting in vesicoscopic reimplantation – Is it needed?: Category: UROLOGY
KokilavaniMSundararajanLakshmiDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Kanchi Kamakoti CHILDS Trust Hospital, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India
E-mail: koksneurocard@gmail.com
Introduction: Ureteric stenting is an integral part of open Cohens reimplant, but not universal in vesicoscopic reimplant. The main concern for stneting is the occurrence of obstruction at the reimplant due to early postoperative edema. We are presenting our experience in this regard.
Methods: Our study includes 15 cases of VUR who underwent vesicoscopic Cohen's reimplantation over a period of 3 years (2019-2022). Demographics, clinical presentation, investigations, management details, hospital stay, and outcome were collected retrospectively from case files.
Results: All these patients, presented with recurrent UTI, who on evaluation had Grade ¾ reflux (9-unilateral and 5-bilateral). 3 patients had associated conditions such as Fanconi's anemia, Duplex system (common sheath reimplant) and posterior urethral valve with single functioning kidney. 12 patients were not stented and 3 patients were stented on the basis of single-functioning kidney with renal impairment. The bladder was catheterized for all and removed on POD-2. Among the 12 nonstented, 11 patients had no complications. 1 patient with recurrent pyelonephritis, lower grade nondilating reflux (with Right-Grade 2, Left-Grade 3) had a temporary period of anuria postoperatively, probably due to reimplant edema and obstruction, which settled within 24 h (while being planned for PCN) without any intervention.
Conclusion: Vesicoscopic reimplantation can be performed safely without stenting, provided there is some degree of ureteric dilatation. VUR with low-grade reflux without significant ureteric dilatation has the chances of ureteric obstruction, where temporary stenting can be protective.
Percutaneous nephrolithotomy – A novel emerging technique for pediatric urolithiasis: Category: UROLOGY
DivyaGaliKundalVijay KumarDebnathPinaki RMeenaAtul KShahShaluSenAmitaDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Atal Bihari Vajpayee Institute of Medical Sciences and Dr. Ram Manohar Lohia Hospital, New Delhi, India
E-mail: galidivya55@gmail.com
Aim: The aim is to study percutaneous nephrolithotomy in detail which is an emerging technique in the management of pediatric urolithiasis
Methods: It is a prospective observational study from October 2021 to March 2022. Children below 15 years with renal stones were included in the study. Patient demographic data; number, site and size of the renal stones were noted preoperatively. PCNL was executed in all patients, either single puncture or double puncture. Postoperative complications were recorded. All patients were followed up for a minimum of 3 months.
Results: A total of 9 patients were included in the study. The mean age of presentation was 9 years. The average size of the stone was 12 mm. Among them two patients had multiple calculi (22%). Majority (n = 7, 77%) of the patients were operated by single puncture. DJ Stenting was done in most of them (n = 7, 77%). During the postoperative period, one patient had a fever and one patient had hematuria. Nephrostomy was kept for 3 days on an average. Residual stones were noted in two patients(n = 2, 22%) who had multiple calyceal stones preoperatively, remaining patients had complete clearance.
Conclusion: Miniaturization of instruments and technologic advances in energy sources has led to the acceptance of PCNL as standard therapy for children with large renal stones and it had a better clearance rate.
Single-stage double-faced transverse prepucial island flap urethroplasty for proximal hypospadias – Our experience: Category: UROLOGY
GopinathV RohitVelmururganRSaravananCAniruthanADepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Institute of Child health/Madras Medical College
E-mail: rogopaedsurg@gmail.com
Aim: To highlight the role and efficacy of single-stage transverse double-faced prepucial island flap urethroplasty in the repair of proximal hypospadias
Materials and Methods: This is a retrospective study conducted between January 2015 and May 2022 including 46 children of age ranging from 1.5 years to 8 years who had undergone single-stage transverse preputial island flap urethroplasty for proximal hypospadias. Details collected included age, associated anomalies, operating time, blood loss, hospital stay, postoperative cosmesis, and functional outcome.
Results: Average follow-up period was 26 months. 80% of the children operated were <5 years of age. Average duration of the single-stage procedure was 206 min. Average duration of hospital stay was 7 days. Complications were recorded. Only 2 children developed an urethrocutaneous fistula post-single-stage procedure.
Conclusion: The use of a double-faced transverse preputial island flap for repair of proximal hypospadias is associated with good functional outcome and cosmesis without the need for multiple surgeries.
Transverse testicular ectopia – Varied presentations: Category: UROLOGY
PrasunaV SaiReddyP SarveshwarRaoN BhuvaneshwaraDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Niloufer Hospital, Hyderabad, Telangana, India
E-mail: drprasunamogili@gmail.com
Objectives: The objective is to describe the varied clinical presentations, and management of transverse testicular ectopia (TTE) presented to the Department of Paediatrics Surgery, Niloufer Hospital.
Methods: Three cases of TTE were discovered accidentally during surgery for an inguinal hernia and undescended testes. One case had an associated persistent Müllerian duct syndrome (PMDS) was associated in 1 case.
Results: Of the three cases, one presented with obstructed inguinal hernia, and other two presented with unilateral hernia with contralateral undescended testis. A surgical approach was chosen and transseptal orchiopexy was performed in all 3 cases. In the case with PMDS, bivalving of the Müllerian structure was done.
Conclusions: TTE is a rare anomaly that usually presents as a unilateral cryptorchidism and a contralateral descendent testis with an associated hernia. In suspected cases, ultrasonographic evaluation may be helpful in diagnosing this condition before surgery. Surgery, transseptal orchiopexy, is highly recommended to manage TTE especially when vasa deferentia are fused. In case of PMDS, it is preferred to preserve Müllerian remnants because of the risk of vas deferens and testicular blood supply damage. Recently, laparoscopy is useful for both diagnosing and management of TTE and associated anomalies.
Prune belly syndrome with different spectrum of urachal anomaly in four cases: Category: UROLOGY
GargDileepSinghUmesh BahadurMathurVinayGroverJitendraDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, G R Medical College, Gwalior, Madhya Pradesh, India
E-mail: singhub.200899@gmail.com
Aims: The aims is to assess the broad spectrum of urachal anomalies in cases of Prune Belly Syndrome
Methods: The medical record of four cases of PBS encountered during the last 4 years, studied and compiled in the form of case series.
Results: Among six cases of PBS presented in the last 4 years, 4 had different severity of urachal anomaly and all had bladder outlet obstruction (three cases had PUV). Three were male and one was female. One case had PBS with a superior vesical fissure (an exstrophy variant) and small deficiency of abdominal wall (most severe). Two cases had patent urachus with urine discharge from umbilicus. The one female case (very rare in female) had the mildest presentation with laxity of abdominal all along with patent urachus and grade III VUR.
Conclusion: Superior vesical fissure can be rarely seen in a neonate born with PBS, which may be a good sign for the immediate survival of patient born with severe congenital anomalies of PBS. The preserved renal functions in these patients may give time window to the clinician for further evaluation and planning of staged management. The patency of urachus gives a new insight about the benefits gained by the child for surviving with such fatal congenital anomalies involving bladder outlet obstruction or PU valve. PBS being very rarely reported in females, more cases need to be studied to make a generalized consensus by summarizing all the available case reports, however, an incomplete form of the prune belly syndrome or pseudo prune belly syndrome is now generally recognized entity and early recognition of symptoms is warranted in view of the subtle presentation of disease.
A case report: Choledochal cyst associated with horseshoe kidney and single system ureter: Category: UROLOGY
SaveMaitreyeeBahetiAkshayKulkarniApoorvaKothariParasDeshmukhShahajiDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, LTMMC and LTMGH, Sion, Maharashtra, India
E-mail: save.maitreyee@gmail.com
Background and Aim: The horseshoe kidney (HSK)is a developmental defect which often occurs at 4–6 weeks of intrauterine life and is the most prevalent renal fusion abnormality, affecting up to 0.25% of the general population. A horseshoe kidney with only one ureter is a rare malformation with unknown etiology and none of the reported cases had associated choledochal cyst. Choledochal cyst is a congenital cystic dilatation of bile duct, occurring anywhere between liver and duodenum, underlying pathogenic mechanism responsible is poorly understood till date. Association of choledochal cyst with horseshoe kidney with a single system ureter seems to be first case to be reported in literature to the best of our knowledge.
Case Presentation: We present the case of 1 month, female antenatally diagnosed case of left hydronephrosis presenting with obstructive jaundice. After investigating with MRCP, computed tomography urography and renal scans, the patient underwent excision of choledochal cyst with hepaticoduodenostomy and cholecystectomy at 1½ months of age, a month later non dismembered Heinicke Mickuliz pyeloplasty for left pelviureteric junction obstruction. Intraoperative findings were horseshoe kidney with right intrarenal pelvis with right small ureter joining left ureter just distal to narrowed left pelviureteric junction and forming single common ureter draining both moieties of horseshoe kidney. Double j stent removed at 6 weeks.
Conclusion: Clinicians should be cautious while approaching a case of HSK. A thorough clinical and radiological assessment should be undertaken as HSK presents a diagnostic and interventional challenge owing to a number of associated anomalies.
Journey of anandam: The surgical history of an abandoned child: Category: UROLOGY
KarmakarDebalinaBasuKalyani SahaVazePoonam GuhaDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, Nil Ratan Sircar Medical College and Hospital, Kolkata, West Bengal, India
E-mail: drdkdebalina@gmail.com
Background: Children with meningomyelocele develop the dreaded complication of neurogenic bladder and is frequently difficult to manage, particularly in the absence of proper care. Here we have attempted to give social continence to an abandoned 8-year-old child.
Case Presentation: A 1-day abandoned male neonate was admitted with high ARM, lipomatous meningomyelocele, unascended kidneys and spinal, cardiac and limb deformities. After initial management of sigmoid colostomy and meningomyelocele repair, he remained unclaimed and became a resident of the ward. He was named Anandam and started growing up under the constant care and vigilance of the department. With time he developed neurogenic bladder, left ureteric reflux, and bilateral paraparesis. CIC was attempted but failed and PSARP could not be done due to undeveloped sphincter muscles. At present he is an 8 years old, healthy, happy, home-schooled intelligent boy. After a distal cologram, MCU, UDS, magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of spine and pelvis he underwent, Abdominoperineal pull through with MACE procedure and formation of catheterizable channel with bladder. At present, he receives MACE washes twice a day and has learnt to do CIC himself and stays dry the rest of the time.
Conclusion: Every year more number of diseased children are being abandoned in hospitals. Their upbringing, education, and treatment remain uncertain as the government maintains reluctance in taking responsibility. With time, patience, and a little of care we, the doctors and health-care workers, can shape their futures, provide them education and some semblance of a normal functioning life, preparing them for the world.
Utility of check cystoscopy after posterior urethral valve fulguration: Category: UROLOGY
BhagatDevprabhaSaxenaRahulDepartment of Pediatric Surgery, AIIMS, Jodhpur, Rajasthan, India
E-mail: devprabhabhagat@gmail.com
Background: Posterior urethral valve (PUV) is a congenital anomaly of the urinary tract which can lead to vesicoureteric reflux, hydronephrosis, and renal dysfunction commonly presenting as voiding symptoms and recurrent UTIs. The management of PUV entails cystoscopic fulguration of valves in the first setting and a follow-up check cystoscopy that is conventionally done 3 months later at most institutes. We aim to study the usefulness of the latter in the identification of residual valves and in the documentation of improvement in the secondary changes due to PUV if any.
Methods: In this retrospective study, electronic medical records were accessed to identify the cases of PUV that underwent cystoscopic fulguration, and data including the symptoms at presentation, ultrasonography findings, and voiding cystourethrogram (VCUG) were collected. All children were treated by cystoscopic fulguration of PUV and were followed clinically for voiding symptoms and with ultrasonography, MCUG, KFT, and DMSA. All patients underwent re-look cystoscopy at 3 months to see residual valves irrespective of their clinical improvement.
Results: The most common symptoms were poor stream, straining at voiding, dribbling of urine, fever, and urinary retention. Residual valves on re-look cystoscopy were seen in 52% of cases, which needed fulguration.
Conclusion: As a significant number of patients were found to have residual valves on Check cystoscopy after 3 months (52%), it is a recommended practice to do the same for diagnosing more cases with residual valves.
